# Decisions — 2026-10

The entries for this month, newest first. The index over all decisions,
including these, is in [`../DECISIONS.md`](../DECISIONS.md); read that first
and come here for the entry you need.

These dated records preserve the reasoning and evidence behind choices and investigations.
The current specification, implementation documents and handoff govern present
work.

## 2026-10-09 — Rebuild a kept lit namespace's own demand rows from its trail at any position, and make no namespace over rows it already holds (Next 4 (bf), slice 19 M19b)

**Status:** accepted (code; no specification change).

**Question:** lit-v1 §10 has a reader resuming kept state rebuild the output set from the kept statements, since no
snapshot root checks it. Area 33's fix ([2026-10-08](#2026-10-08--resume-a-kept-lit-namespace-only-where-no-output-row-lies-past-its-tip-and-derive-lits-outputs-once-audit-area-33))
rebuilt the output rows and checked each demand the trail stands up against its row. It left (bf): a settlement derives
its output and nullifiers from its demand's stored row, so a namespace's own demand row that no trail record stood up
can create an output. Must the rebuild cover the demand rows too, and which other rows?

**Decision:**
1. `keptOutputsHold` (reader.ts) requires a namespace's own demand rows through the checked position to be exactly the
   demands the trail stood up, each at its record's position, and at a tip none past it. A demand identity has one row
   per namespace, so every row matched, as many as were stood up, is equality. A demand or output row whose position
   (or leaf) is not an integer matches none. The known-prefix fast path keeps the tip condition by one indexed lookup.
2. A first walk of a namespace reads all of its own output and demand rows, with no lower bound: a row at position 0
   or below, or below leaf 0, is visible to the state, so it is judged too. Later walks read past what is known.
3. A namespace number must hold no output or demand row before its namespace is made (`open`, `copyFrontier`), and a
   demand identity no nullifier or end row before its demand stands (`append`, and `copyFrontier` for each demand it
   copies; a statement identity stands once): either is `KeptStateMismatch`, so a read discards and reads again, and
   an operator journal, whose rows are its state, refuses `STORAGE`. A stored key that is not 32 bytes is the same. Discarding a kept file clears every namespace
   table, so rows under no namespace go with the rest. This covers the namespaces a process replays itself, which no
   kept-state check reads.
4. What the rebuild knows of a store's namespaces is forgotten whenever its rows may have gone (`generation`: a
   discard, a collection, a rolled-back walk, or a transaction SQLite rolled back itself), so a number made again is
   never vouched for by an earlier walk. A replay records what it wrote only once its savepoint stands, so a refused
   checkpoint's rollback forgets nothing.
5. Replay schema 12 indexes `demand` by namespace and position, so each check reads only its range and every seek above
   is indexed. A schema-11 file is discarded and replayed; an operator journal moves to profile `pool-store/v3/9`, so
   one made before refuses to open rather than run without the index, as at every profile change.
6. The other rows stay vouched for by §14's whole-file digest, as the pool's are (pool-v3 §14, "the digest vouches for
   its other rows"): demand ends, demand tag index rows, nullifiers' tags and totals below the tip. §2's output
   derivation reads only outputs, demands and demands' nullifiers, all checked above; a damaged other row can still
   change a verdict through a lock or a demand's end, as for the pool.

**Rationale:**
- The defect sits in the reader's kept-state rebuild, not the lit wallet as WORK.md first placed it. The wallet's
  seed-derived acceptance keys remain a separate Next 8 cleanup for when `src/lit/holdings.ts` next changes.
- Alternatives: re-deriving demand rows from event rows does not help, since a phantom row can sit at any event's
  position; counting a namespace's rows without the index scans the table at each resumed checkpoint; keeping demands
  only in memory costs a full replay on reopen, which (bb) and (bg) exist to avoid. For item 3, clearing a new number's
  rows from every table at allocation scans the tables whose keys do not lead with the namespace; two seeks over the
  tables §10 rebuilds, with the discard clearing the rest, cost nothing per namespace.
- Reaching any of these needs a damaged kept file whose digest was recorded again (as for area 33's output rows); the
  fix makes a resumed lit verdict equal a full replay's for the rows §10 names.

**Evidence:**
- Three tests in `test/lit-reader.test.ts`, each with the operator's own state made to read the damaged row, so its
  snapshot includes the act and only the kept-state check can tell: a demand of Mallory's that no record stood up, at
  positions -1, 0, the kept tip and one past it, then settled; the same demand under the namespace numbers a successor
  takes next; an output row at leaf -1, then spent. On main the first and third fail at the journal's reopening check
  (`storedTipHolds` true) and the second's kept read accepts the settlement; with the fix each kept read is discarded and
  refuses as full replay does (`DEMAND`, `INPUT`). The parent commit's own fix already failed the first test at -1 and 0.
- Review (a fresh adversarial reviewer, on the first commit): no finding inside the walked range; two blockers
  reproduced, a phantom at position 0 or below (F1) and orphan rows under the next namespace number (F2), and a major
  carried over from area 33, an output row below leaf 0 (F3): items 2 and 3. Minors taken: a non-integer position
  (F4, item 1), a journal made before the index (F5, item 5), and the known map surviving a rollback (F6, an evidence
  gap, item 4). The reviewer's honest cases (demands settled in their segment and in a successor, lower checks then
  the tip) kept their state with the fix.
- Read-back (a second fresh reviewer, on the rework): F1–F4 and F6 closed; two majors taken. A demand's nullifier
  row held before the demand stands joined it when the replay stood it up and excluded an honest checkpoint with no
  discard (item 3; item 6's first wording missed that nullifier rows feed §2). Bumping the generation at every
  savepoint rollback made each refused checkpoint rebuild every kept namespace again, 810 rows over 9 exclusions in
  a 200-record read (item 4). A minor taken: an output row's non-integer position threw (item 1). Two tests fail
  before these and pass after: the nullifier row at the kept tip and under the next numbers, and five excluded
  checkpoints in a 200-record read, which now walk no namespace from its first row (five first walks before).
- Targeted read-back (a third fresh reviewer, on those fixes): no false positive of item 3 in honest replay (a statement
  identity is refused again in its own namespace by `REPEATED_STATEMENT` and across imports by recovery's
  `isEffective`), and the known-walk lifetime sound. One major taken: `copyFrontier`, the journal's import path, copied
  a demand's nullifier rows without item 3's check, so a stray row joined the copy and a settlement of it spent the
  stray nullifier (a test in `test/lit-state.test.ts` fails before). Minors taken: a malformed stored key, and the
  journal surfacing a mismatch unnamed; a nit taken: a transaction SQLite rolled back itself.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-09 — One loopback listener and one serve frame for the operator, a replica and a relay (simplify)

**Status:** accepted (code and documentation; no specification change).

**Question:** slice 12 built the transport in three steps (M12a operator's holders' listener, M12b replica, M12c
relay), and each step copied what came before it. What does the serving layer need once, and which work since the last
simplify run (`09534a8`) no longer pays its way?

**Decision:**
1. **One listener** (`service-http.ts` `listener`): it refuses a non-loopback peer `LOCAL_ONLY` before any handler runs,
   maps a thrown request to its refusal, and holds one set of lifetime bounds. The operator's, a replica's and a relay's
   services supply only their credential check and routes. The client's URL check (`listenerUrl`) is likewise one, for
   `V3ServiceClient` and `sendToRelay`.
2. **One serve frame** (`src/cli/serve.ts`): flags, the onion-host check, listening with the port-in-use refusal, stop on
   signals, close, token files and the holder-file parser, shared by `operator serve`, `reader serve` and `relay serve`.
   One packer and one read statement set serve the journal's and a replica's parts.
3. **One change in behaviour:** a replica's round reads its frontier through the same read as `supply`. A file another
   process holds is now that backing's `STORAGE` refusal in the round, and `serve` keeps serving. Before, it ended
   `serve`, though `roundRefusal`'s own contract names that case.
4. **Retired or cut** (#168): the framer's hostile-input node equivalence harness, whose decision was recorded
   2026-09-24 and whose report bound `dist` files later commits changed; M11c1–M11c3's superseded probe text; and
   WORK.md's delivered items and figures stated away from their homes. The offline transfer check runs in
   `check:scripts`, not in a real-proof group.

**Rationale:**
- Every refusal code, status, message, check order, bound, event and file written stays as it was, so the copies held
  no distinct behaviour.
- The source lines come out about even: the shared module documents once what three copies left undocumented. The gain
  is one place for the credential gate's order and the serve lifecycle, which audit area 30 and M12a–c each had to
  check three times.
- Declined in this run, as not clearly simpler or not verifiable now:
  - the journal's own fork pruning in `store.parts()`, which would trade memory for a concept;
  - the wallet's paired pool and `keyed*` flows and lit's seed-derived acceptance keys, which are custody code for
    their next change (Next 8; (bf) touches the lit wallet next);
  - one `moe` harness for the two command drills (Next 8).

**Evidence:**
- Focused suites passed: service-http, service-client, service-onion, lit-service, relay, replica, evidence-store,
  store and cli. The new test covers the port-in-use refusal and the `--onion` check.
- The service check and package check passed, as did the lit command drill, which runs all three `serve` commands
  behind onion names. CI ran the full suite.
- An independent adversarial review found no blocker or major issue. Its minor findings are resolved:
  - queued journal work is now awaited before closing on a failed holders' listen;
  - the flag check order is kept;
  - a comment typo is fixed.
- **Gap:** no test drives a replica round into a held file. The change is availability-only, and the reviewer traced
  every source of `FileInUse` in a round.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-09 — State the release's security argument, take the request digest as its authentication channel, and give every gate's open item an owner (slice 19 M19a)

**Status:** accepted (documentation and gate standing; no specification or code change).

**Question:** the "Private, sound payments" gate asks for an independently reviewed security argument, and none was
written ([direction 2026-10-09](#2026-10-09--fix-the-operators-restart-replay-before-the-10-run-and-write-the-security-argument-the-external-review-needs),
item 3). What does the release claim, on which assumptions, and why do the checks imply it? Three further questions
came with it:
- (c) Which channel authenticates a payment request to a person? "Usable payment" listed a qualified human
  authentication channel as open.
- (d) Which gate items have no owner?
- Does writing the argument find a defect?

**Decision:**
1. **[`docs/SECURITY_ARGUMENT.md`](../docs/SECURITY_ARGUMENT.md)** states five claims for pool-v3 on Ergo: supply,
   payment soundness, payment privacy, holder authorization, and compartmentalized failure and recovery. Lit notes are
   a short section. It lists sixteen assumptions, each with what it protects and what follows if it fails. Each claim
   gives its assumptions, the argument from rule to code and test, and its residual.
   - The adversary is every other party at once, colluding.
   - The operator is trusted for service only, never for validity, since every judging party replays.
   - What leaves a compartment is named: the proof system and parameters, the venue, a shared operator, and the
     release's own code.
2. **(c) The request channel is the payer's own authentic carrier for the full 32-byte digest, compared by machine.**
   The protocol supplies no carrier, and no format could. Its limit is stated: a substituted carrier pays the
   substituter, undetected.
   - Next 9's text/QR frame stays a convenience over the same digest.
   - A request signed under a stable receiver key is declined, since it links a receiver's requests (C4.3).
   - A key kept per payer relationship is left as a later candidate. It links only within a relationship the payer
     knows, and buys continuity after first contact. But first contact needs the same authentic carrier, and it adds a
     signed message and per-relationship custody.
   - An operator's fee request follows the same rule. A substituted one diverts the fee and shows the payment's
     backing.
   - "Usable payment" now names the channel as decided. Device custody stays open with provisioning authority.
3. **(d) Every gate's open item has an owner or is corrected.**
   - *Defined profile:* deployment choices are each backer's, declared in **E** (pool-v3 §1: adoption "is not a
     deployment"). This is an exclusion, not open work.
   - *Adversarial model:* the runtime now runs each modelled mechanism with real proofs and signatures. Absence
     at the venue is authenticated by exhaustion, while a withheld trail is nobody's provable act (§C4); its evidence is the unresolved verdict plus the replica. This is a correction.
   - *Public supply:* `moe reader supply` is the product. Its mainnet use waits with mainnet. This is a correction.
   - *Durable operation, Available state, Practical deployment, Release assurance:* each open item names slice 18,
     the retained boundaries (provisioning, mainnet) or the Open questions (the external review).
4. **New: Next 4 (bh).** The commitment's signed message (pool-v3 §13.1: `"moe/commitment/v2" ‖ sequence ‖ root`)
   names no venue. An operator key used on two venues therefore lets anyone copy its commitments between them.
   - One copied commitment ends that venue's journal service for good (`CONFLICT`).
   - A copy with a higher sequence makes the venue drop the operator's later, lower ones (C2.3.3), and receipts
     naming them lapse.
   - The two venues' commitments at one sequence are equivocation evidence, copied or not.
   - `moe operator init` draws a fresh key per directory and takes none from outside, so the reference's own use is
     safe. The argument states the duty as A15, and the protocol rules as party duty 12. A venue-bound commitment
     context is a v4 candidate.
5. **New assumption A16: the venue's anchor is a header of the real chain.** Its context is authenticated by linkage
   alone and supplied by one supplier, and the operator or backer chooses it. A fabricated anchor would hand its maker
   the whole venue. Mainnet's floor on the anchor's difficulty stays Next 4 (ad), with the slice that enables
   mainnet. The release runs only on reference venues, and the argument says so, and says that on the testnet
   finality rests on the reader's own nodes.

**Rationale:**
- *A claim per invariant of the intent.* The workspace intent names open entry, independent verification, private
  payments, public supply verification, holder authorization and compartmentalized failure. The five claims are the
  last five; open entry is the terms' own and needs no security claim. A claim per rule would repeat the protocol
  rules' map without stating why the rules together suffice.
- *Cite, do not restate.* The visibility matrix is the privacy argument already, and the argument summarizes it and
  links it. Copying it would make two homes for one measurement of leakage.
- *A text/QR frame for (c):* this was considered as the channel. It encodes the frame or the digest. It does not make a
  carrier authentic, so it changes nothing the argument relies on.
- *Leave the gate items open until the external review:* that would leave items with no owner, which the direction
  check counted as a gap. The corrections make no item easier than it is. Each removes text that later work had made
  untrue, or names who owns what remains.

**Evidence:** sources read are listed in the argument's links. They include Construction §C0, §C1, §C2b, §C3 and §C4,
pool-v3 §§1–4, §7, §11.3 and §13, venue-ergo §§2–3 and §9, pool-recovery C3.5, the matrix, the protocol rules and the
release record. Code was read at the claims' steps: `state.ts` `judgmentOf`, `construction.ts`'s v3 view,
`recovery.ts` `locked`, `scope-reader.ts`, `keys.ts` `verifySignatureStrict`, `venue-records.ts`'s commitment message
and `cli/operator.ts` `init`. A read-only inventory mapped each primitive to the objects it protects: libraries,
signed messages and their contexts, hashes, capsule and backup ciphers, randomness, and the proof backend.

*Review:* a fresh adversarial reviewer read the argument, the gate edits and the code. It found no blocker. Every
finding was taken:
- **Major:** the scope described a mainnet venue the guard refuses (now a "Where it runs today" paragraph, with the
  testnet's finality resting on the reader's own nodes).
- **Major:** anchor authenticity was unstated, and (ad) was omitted (now A16).
- **Major:** adoption mode was missing from the supply induction. Adoption skips proof, input and recovery checks
  and rests on the block being force-judged publications (now step 8, and claim 4's recovery).
- **Major:** the privacy property was stronger than the matrix. The payer's and K's openings, the tags of requests and
  demands, and the payee's view were missing, as were the sync and submit join on one circuit and the matrix's open
  duties (the property is now qualified and its disclosures listed).
- **Minor:**
  - Equivocation was said to be acted on. It is evidence only, the journal's `CONFLICT` is its one consumer, and §C4's
    residual is now quoted.
  - (bh)'s impact lacked the journal's `CONFLICT` and the lapsed receipts, and its `init` lever changed nothing.
  - A copied revocation's residual was missing.
  - Claim 5 lacked A1–A4 and A15, and claim 2's "once" lacked the one canonical carrier (term chain, `IMPORT_RANK`,
    retirement).
  - (c) lacked fee requests and the per-relationship key.
  - The Adversarial model row overstated availability and cited no tests.
- **Nits:** the replacement's signers, lit settlement's authorization, the substitution tests' names, rho's HMAC
  (A7), and (bf) in the lit section.

The reviewer found these sound:
- prefix-free contexts, every other signed message binding the configuration and a segment or demand;
- the pinned versions, strict Ed25519, the parameter and key checks;
- the cited refusal codes, locks, capsule and backup ciphers, randomness and wallet checks;
- that no issue or spend reaches adoption;
- the cited invariants and §C4 rows, every cited file, test and anchor, and the gate owners.

A second fresh reviewer read back the fixes and confirmed each finding closed against the code. It found five minors
and four nits, all taken:
- lit-v1's pin is `80a4ea1`, not `e7f7f24`;
- the privacy exception now covers the property's first sentence;
- the decision's own availability wording now matches the gate row;
- `IMPORT_RANK` is described exactly: a takeover's checkpoints of another operator, and a continuation's held
  opening;
- a dropped commitment excludes its continuation;
- the synthetic and local venues are named beside the testnet;
- rho rests on A7;
- the open duties cited are items 2–3;
- "Where it runs today" became a heading.

## 2026-10-09 — Record a kept file's digest before its log moves in, and keep a long read's progress by time (Next 4 (bb))

**Status:** accepted (implementation of pool-v3 §14's kept-file check and the storage decision's keep points; no
specification change).

**Question:** slice 18's 10⁶ run stopped at 1.25·10⁴ statements. After a deliberate stop, every restarted `serve`
spent its first admission replaying the journal's whole history (Next 4 (bb), [direction](#2026-10-09--fix-the-operators-restart-replay-before-the-10-run-and-write-the-security-argument-the-external-review-needs)).
Slice 15's restart at 10⁵ had cost 1.5 s. What makes a restart replay everything, and how does a restart read only what
it has not kept, at any history length?

**Findings** ([probe](../docs/POOL_DEPLOYMENT_PROBES.md#the-restart-read-next-4-bb)):
- **A stop between a keep point's commit and its digest discarded the whole kept file.** The journal's own reads keep
  their state in `journal.db.reads`, vouched for by `journal.db.reads.sha256` (§14: "the whole kept state must match one
  SHA256 digest held in storage only the reader writes"). A keep point committed into the write-ahead log, moved the log
  into the file and only then recorded the digest. A process stopped in between left a file whose digest was the old
  one. The next opening found no match, discarded the file as §14 requires, and the next read replayed the history.
  The storage decision had accepted this ("a crash rolls back to the last keep point"), but the rollback it named
  never happened: the log's commit was already in the file.
- *Reproduced:* serve killed in that window at 2,339 statements. The restart replayed all of them in its first
  admission (about 100 s). A stop at an idle moment kept the reads (first read 2.05 s at 2.6·10³, 2.4 s at 6·10³).
- *The 10⁶ run met it:* its reads file had been deleted and made again (an NTFS file number outside the block of the
  run's other files, with the old creation time carried over), which is what a failed opening leaves. With unchanged
  code and an intact file, no other route discards the journal's kept reads. The window opens about once a checkpoint
  for 18–120 ms, so it is met rarely; that the 10⁶ run's stop fell in it is inferred from the file, not observed.
- **A replay that is stopped kept nothing,** because keep points came only every 10,000 records (minutes). The keeper
  killed each restart within about 18 s, so each began again. The storage decision's item 6 had asked for keep points
  at "an interval (count or time)"; only the count was built.
- **Opening hashes the whole file** (about 190 MB/s on the PC, about 12 s at 10⁶), and the journal opened its reads
  file at its first read: in the first admission's turn.
- Next 4 (bb)'s first description, a read at an index below the kept read's, is the kept walk's rule: such a read
  runs on a temporary walk. The journal reads only at its own clock, which a restart cannot move back (the journal
  refuses a venue behind what it observed), so service never takes that path.

**Decision:**
1. **The keep point records the new digest before the log moves in.** After its commit, it reads the log's committed
   frames as SQLite's recovery does: the header's salts and cumulative checksum, through the last commit frame. It
   hashes their page images into the page digest at the commit's page count, records that digest, and then moves the
   log into the file. The digest is computed from the log, so the file is not read again (as M11b12, from the log's
   images rather than the moved-in pages).
2. **An opening drops a log that no digest vouches for.** If the log holds a commit and the file as it lies (before
   recovery) still gives the recorded digest, the log and its index are removed. The state is then the one last
   vouched for, and only the interrupted keep point's work is read again. Otherwise the opening is as before: recovery
   moves the log in, and the file must give the recorded digest or is discarded. A stop after the digest is recorded
   is therefore recovered. A copied file (another identity) or a damaged one is still discarded.
3. **A walk also keeps by time:** once a record has replayed and 5 s have passed since it opened or last kept, as
   well as every 10,000 records. A read shorter than 5 s commits only at its close, as before.
4. **`serve` opens the journal's kept reads before it listens** (`openReads`), so the whole-file check is not paid in
   the first admission. A journal that has never read keeps no file and opens none.

**Rationale:**
- *Keep the digest after the checkpoint, but accept a log whose commit it does not name:* that would reuse state no
  digest vouches for, which §14 forbids.
- *Return to a rollback journal:* SQLite writes the file in place before its commit point, and that point lies inside
  one call, so the digest still could not precede the commit. The log also names the changed pages, which keeps
  M11b12's cost proportional to the change.
- *An undo copy of the changed pages before the checkpoint:* a second write path for the same guarantee, with more
  input and output per keep point.
- *Remember verified proofs across processes,* so that any whole replay is cheap: declined in slice 15 (the rows
  would become the reason). It would not have prevented the discard.
- The log's parser follows SQLite's file format (§4): a checksum or salt mismatch ends the committed frames, as it ends
  recovery. If the parser and SQLite ever disagreed, the next opening's digest check would fail and the file would be
  discarded, never trusted.

**Evidence:**
- *Tests,* each failing on main:
  - the replay store reopens a keep point stopped before its digest as the digest names it (main discards it). The
    same test covers a stop after the digest (recovered), a torn commit (nothing committed), a damaged file beside an
    unvouched log (discarded) and a copied file (discarded);
  - a restarted journal whose reads stopped there verifies 2 proofs in its first admission, the record the last
    process admitted and its own (main: 14, the whole history);
  - a 6 s-a-proof read keeps by time before its close, and a short read does not.
- *The fixed build under slice 18's driver,* at 6.1·10³ statements: killed after the commit and before the digest, the
  restart opened its reads in 0.15 s and read in 2.2 s; killed after the digest and before the checkpoint, 0.15 s and
  1.8 s. [Probe](../docs/POOL_DEPLOYMENT_PROBES.md#the-restart-read-next-4-bb).
- *The wallet crash check* (`check:pool:v3-wallet`) exits a read between its replay file's commit and its digest. It
  now verifies the new record only (1, not 2 before).
- *Review:* a fresh adversarial reviewer took f113b3a..7a32aab and found no blocker or major.
  - It walked every stop point around a keep point.
  - It checked the log parser against SQLite's recovery: byte order, header and frame checksums, salts, commit
    frames, a page size of 65536.
  - A probe of its own compared the parser's committed pages with the checkpointed file over nine workloads (schema
    creation, growth and shrinking, spilled and rolled-back transactions, overflow pages, several commits): no
    mismatch.
  - Taken:
    - an opening that drops a log no longer hashes the file twice;
    - `serve` leaves a reads file another handle holds to its first read (BUSY, retried) rather than failing to start;
    - `apply` checks its log before it changes any hash.
  - Its read-back confirmed these and found one problem in the first. A rollback journal placed beside the file
    (tampering; a crash leaves none beside a kept file) is played into it when it opens, after the hash taken before
    opening. So that hash is reused only when no rollback journal is there; otherwise the file is hashed again after
    it opens, and a test injects such a journal (it failed without the guard).
  - Recorded as limits:
    - on POSIX, an opening can unlink the log of a live second handle. The kept file it then corrupts fails the next
      digest check and is discarded. This exposure predates the fix (a mismatch removes every file), Windows refuses
      the removal, and a directory runs one `moe` process at a time;
    - the digest file's rename is not fsynced through its directory, so a power loss can lose a vouched state, which
      is then discarded;
    - the test's crash image is written by another connection after a close, not left by a killed keep point (the
      end-to-end kills above cover that).
  - Evidence gap it named: with real proofs, reads over 5 s now keep by time. CI's real-proof groups run them.
- *Limits:* stand-in proofs and one host, as in slice 15. The first admission after a restart still reads what the
  previous process admitted since its last kept read, 1.5–2.6 s here at 42 statements a checkpoint (slice 15's open
  limit). A whole replay still runs inside an admission's turn when the kept reads are discarded for good reason: a
  new code version names another kept context (§14), or a file is damaged or copied. At 10⁶ that is hours (Next 4 (bg)).

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-09 — Fix the operator's restart replay before the 10⁶ run, and write the security argument the external review needs

**Status:** accepted (order and scope of WORK.md's Next, and the release gates' standing; no specification or code change).

**Question:** since the [previous direction check](#2026-10-07--exercise-lits-failure-path-and-settle-7s-acceptance-owner-before-adoption-and-give-evidence-availability-a-replica-in-slice-12)
(reference-ts fc6b27d), slices 12, 13 and 14 closed, slice 15 brought the design point's two edges within budget, slices
16 and 17 probed Bitcoin, audit 33 and Next 4 (bc), (be) and (bd) fixed lit and wallet defects, and slice 18, the 10⁶ run,
started on the local machine. The release-assurance slice is done but for the external review, which lies outside the
standing authorization. Is the remaining work on the shortest path to the release target? Does any gate lack an owner?

**Findings:**
- **The 10⁶ run is stopped, and a restarted `moe operator serve` read for minutes at 1.25·10⁴ statements.**
  - The run was killed for a restart test at 12,489 statements. In each of the ten restarts after 00:25 UTC, the
    client's first submission outlasted its 10 s idle bound. The keeper gave up, which the handoff did not report.
  - One read was timed to its end: a probe this run let the driver wait instead of failing. That restarted `serve`
    held its turn about 5⅓ minutes (00:49:15–00:54:46, about 640 s of the process's CPU).
  - By then the restarts' mined blocks (36 `SCHEDULE` retries against a silence of 32) had carried the scope past its
    witnessing horizon (`SCHEDULE`, "the witnessing horizon reaches silence"), so `serve` can neither admit nor commit.
  - This matches (bb)'s symptom, a whole replay in one turn. Slice 15 met (bb) only through a read before `serve`
    listens, and Next 4 filed it as performance. The root cause is unconfirmed here: slice 15's own restart at 10⁵
    took 1.5 s, and the probe's restart followed ten interrupted reads.
  - If a restart's read grows with the history, a restart near 10⁶ would hold admission for hours. That is against the
    operator's budget ("Restart resumes from its journal without re-verifying retained history", and admission ≤ 1 s),
    and a backing whose silence window is shorter than the read lapses because its operator restarted. It would be a
    durable-operation failure, and slice 18 cannot measure a restart until the cause is known and fixed.
- **One gate's requirement has no work toward it.** "Private, sound payments" asks for "an independently reviewed
  security argument". No document states one. Construction §C4 is a threat model, and the protocol rules map
  rule → code → test. Neither states the release's claims, the assumptions each rests on and why the checks imply
  them. Examples of such assumptions are the proof system's soundness, one honest contributor to the parameters,
  Poseidon2, SHA-256 and Ed25519, and the venue's honest majority. On Ergo that last one holds against a measured
  concentration ([venue research](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md)). The external review is the last
  release-assurance item, and without such an argument it has no target to check. Writing it is within authority, and
  it shortens the review.
- **The gates' open lists disagree with the smallest profile.**
  - "Usable payment" lists cancellation and statements spending several backings as open. Next 9 holds both "only
    when a gate needs them", and the smallest profile needs neither. Cancellation is the wallet's "release with other
    outputs", not one of C3.6's acts. Release and withdrawal are implemented.
  - "Practical deployment" calls mobile and whole-browser peak memory "unmeasured", and an unmeasured item fails a
    gate. Yet a phone wallet is outside the target, and the package declares no browser target.
  - "A qualified human authentication channel" for a request's digest is open, with no Next item beyond Next 9's
    text/QR frame.
- **Drift is small.** Slices 16 and 17 serve a second venue after release. They were cheap and read-only, and their
  next step needs funds, so they stop by themselves. Lit's fixes corrected an adopted profile, which is right before an
  external review. Lit's packet-budget measurement (Next 10) and (az) serve no release gate, yet the handoff proposed
  (az) and `venue-bitcoin.md` beside (bb) and (bf).
- **The public pages lag.** The site's reference line still names "statements spending several backings" as
  remaining, and it says nothing of the commands, lit notes, the replica or the release record. The organization
  profile says the wire formats can change, though pool-v3 and lit-v1 are adopted by name.

**Decision:**
1. **(bb) is next, as a fix.** The first step confirms the mechanism, or files a new letter. The restarted read in
   `scratch/dp6/run` reproduces it, and a smaller history shows whether it grows.
   - *Acceptance:* a restarted `serve`, after an abrupt stop too, reads only what it has not kept. Its first admission
     costs what slice 15 measured after a restart (about 1–1.5 s at 42 statements a checkpoint, the previous process's
     admissions read once), whatever the history length.
   - That restart cost above 1 s is slice 15's recorded limit and stays open in Next 4; this fix
     does not widen into it.
   - Evidence: a regression test at a depth where a whole replay shows, failing on main, and a journal adversarial
     review (restart, fencing and kept reads are sensitive). Under the journal's audit area, as Next 4 asked.
   - The Durable operation gate names it until then.
2. **Slice 18 restarts from scratch on the fixed revision.** The stopped run is past its witnessing horizon and is
   discarded: continuing would need a return to a new segment, which the measurement must not contain. Two driver
   changes:
   - it keeps waiting through a client timeout without mining (this run's patch);
   - its terms' silence is set far beyond any restart's mined blocks, so that a measurement never ends on the probe's
     own clock. Silence is a terms parameter, not a measured cost.

   Its acceptance gains the restart's first admission at 10⁵ and 10⁶. While it runs the PC stays light.
3. **Slice 19, readiness for the external review, starts now in cloud runs, beside (bb).** It is documentation and
   touches no file (bb) does, and the external review it prepares is the slowest item to arrange.
   - (a) A security argument (`docs/SECURITY_ARGUMENT.md`). It covers the release's claims: supply, payment soundness
     and privacy, holder authorization, and compartmentalized failure with its recovery. For each claim it gives the
     assumptions and the checks that imply it, and the residual risks, and it gets a fresh adversarial reviewer.
     - It builds on what exists: Construction §C4's threat model, pool-v3's soundness note (the key bytes, one
       destroyed contribution), the visibility matrix's privacy statements, and the protocol rules' rule-to-test map.
     - Assumptions include the proof system and Barretenberg's implementation of it, one honest parameter contributor
       (and genuine powers for zero knowledge), Poseidon2, SHA-256, Ed25519, AES-GCM and HKDF for capsules, and the
       venue's honest majority.
     - Residual risks include Ergo's measured concentration, which the venue research already bounds to development,
       testnet and small-value use, and C3.5's late-witnessed release (cancellation not built).
   - (b) A review brief: scope, the pinned release record, commands to reproduce, the dispositioned findings, and what
     is out of scope.
   - (c) A decision on the human authentication channel. Either state the digest argument as the release's channel,
     with its limit, or take Next 9's text request frame.
   - (d) Reconcile each gate's standing. Every open item gets an owner (a Next item, an Open question or a stated
     exclusion), or is corrected where later work closed it. Candidates: Adversarial model's "complete runtime
     integration and authenticated availability evidence", Public supply's "supported supply-verification product"
     (`moe reader supply` and the replica exist), and Defined profile's "deployment choices".

   (bf), a small correctness fix in an adopted profile, comes before the brief is frozen.
4. **The public pages are refreshed after slice 19.** That covers the site and the organization profile, with the
   site's push double-check.
5. **Gate corrections now:**
   - "Usable payment" moves cancellation and multi-backing statements out of its open list, as outside the smallest
     profile;
   - "Practical deployment" says that a phone wallet and a browser target lie outside the target. The budgets section
     records the browser as a scope choice, as it does the phone;
   - "Private, sound payments" names the missing argument;
   - "Durable operation" names the restart read.
6. **Demoted:** (az) waits on its trigger (a holder past about 1.5·10⁵ unspent notes); `venue-bitcoin.md` waits for
   funding and the release; lit's packet-budget measurement waits for the release.
7. The estimate moves to about **72% (62–80%)**. The restart read is a gate defect not counted before, and the
   security argument is remaining work not counted before. The gate corrections remove nothing that had been counted.

**Rationale:**
- *Relaunch slice 18 now with the waiting driver, and fix (bb) beside it:* this measures the steady state sooner. But
  each restart could then cost a long replay, and the fix's own checks (unit suite, local replay) would load the PC
  while it measures. The restart, which the run exists to measure, would also be measured on code about to change.
- *Treat (bb) as a slice 18 limit:* an operator crash could then lapse its backings at scale. That would cross the
  budget declared before testing and compartmentalized failure, so it cannot be a limit.
- *Leave the security argument to the external reviewer:* the reviewer would reconstruct the claims and assumptions
  from the specification, at the slowest and costliest point. Errors in an argument the project wrote are findings
  too.
- *Put the argument in the specification repository:* its claims are the reference's release claims, made over this
  implementation's checks and the gate it serves. It cites Construction for the protocol's own claims. Moving it later
  costs a link.
- *Raise Ergo's concentration as a question of intent:* the venue research already bounds Ergo to development, testnet
  and small-value pilots and names the two remedies (a second venue profile, a venue-moving record). Material value on
  mainnet needs funds and a release, both outside the standing authorization. The argument states the risk; nothing
  in the intent changes.
- *Close the remaining gates by provisioning:* qualified custody and storage, mainnet funds and the external review
  each need authority outside this repository's. They stay where they are: the external review and mainnet funding for
  Bitcoin's probe in Open questions, and custody, storage and mainnet in the retained boundaries. The order here puts
  the work inside authority first.
- No line of work is dropped.

**Evidence:**
- *Scope:* WORK.md and the log at eafa5a3. That is reference-ts fc6b27d..eafa5a3: 159 commits without merges
  (13 `feat`, 46 `fix`, 71 `docs` of which 14 are report re-recordings, 23 `test`, 6 `chore`). The specification range
  is 3af6abe..46e6933. The site and the organization profile have no change.
- *The run:* `scratch/dp6/driver.log`, `keep-out.log` and `exit.txt` ("gave up after 10 quick failures"), and
  `run/report.json` (ten `AbortError`s at 12,489 statements; waits: 36 `SCHEDULE`, 21 client timeouts).
- *The probe:* `run/serve.log` and the probe's log `scratch/dp6/restart-probe.log` (client timeouts logged as attempts
  3–23, then "the witnessing horizon reaches silence"), and `run/serve-samples.jsonl` (642 s of the process's CPU).
- *Sources read:* the production requirements' gates, Construction §C4, pool-recovery C3.6, and the wallet guide's term
  for cancellation.
- CI on eafa5a3 is green. This is documentation only.
- *Review:* a fresh adversarial reviewer checked the claims against these sources. It found no blocker and the
  following, all taken:
  - Major: the relaunch steps named a Startup entry text that no file held (now in `launch.cmd`'s comment).
  - Major: the restart read was attributed to (bb) as fact and to every restart (now matched to its symptom, with
    the cause to confirm).
  - Major: gate items without an owner (item 3 (d), and the Durable operation row).
  - Minor: slice 19's start, now beside (bb).
  - Minor: (bb)'s acceptance against slice 15's restart cost.
  - Minor: the argument's existing parts and missing assumptions.
  - Minor: the browser as a recorded scope choice, and C3.5's late release as a residual risk.
  - Minor: Ergo's concentration (rationale above).
  - Nits: counts, the horizon rather than a silence edge, the locations of the provisioning items, and rotating
    `driver.log` before the relaunch.
  - A read-back of the fixes closed eight. It found two new inaccuracies, both fixed: a restart's 1–1.5 s first
    admission had been called an accepted exception, and the gate row stated the lapse as a fact rather than a live
    venue's risk.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-08 — Pay a failed payment's request again only by a retry that conflicts with it (Next 4 (bd))

**Status:** accepted after review (below). No specification change: which payments a wallet builds is the wallet's rule;
lit-v1 §8 has the payee credit one output per request and pool-v3's doors refuse an output already created.

**Question:** a saved payment that failed kept its request's output (pool) or key (lit) reserved for good, so the request
could not be paid again under another alias; the holder had to ask the payee for a new request. Releasing the reservation
alone could pay twice if a failed record were ever admitted, and the resolution code keeps that door open
(`resolve` moves `failed` to `final` on evidence). What lets a retry pay the request without that risk?

**Decision:**
1. A request held only by failed saved payments may be paid again under another alias. Every other saved payment of it (prepared
   or final) still refuses (`CONFLICT`, "request is already in a saved payment").
2. Pool-v3: the retry pays the request's exact output, as the failed payment did, and every door refuses an output already
   created (`OUTPUT`, state.ts `checkUniqueEffects`), so at most one of them is admitted, however the failure was judged. Its own
   change and zero outputs are fresh. `saved_outputs` is keyed by output and alias (profile `moe/wallet/v3/11`).
3. Lit-v1: a request names only a key, and an output derives from its statement's nullifiers (§2), so the retry must spend a note
   of each failed payment of the request, unless one of that payment's inputs is spent in canonical history (its outputs enter
   with its nullifiers and it is not final, so no door admits it). One whose outputs are all in canonical history is final and the
   request paid (`CONFLICT`); one none of whose notes is available (reserved, locked or spent only with force) refuses
   (`CONFLICT`, "sync until it is decided"). Selection is `select`'s rule over the selections meeting those sets; where only
   the rule blocks a covering selection, the refusal is `CONFLICT`, not `FUNDS`. A spend repeating a saved statement byte for
   byte refuses (`CONFLICT`, naming the remedy) instead of failing the database's uniqueness. A lit request is its backing and
   key, so both checks read the backing's payments only (profile `moe/wallet/keyed/4`).
4. A backup or handoff whose non-failed payments share a request's output (pool) or a payee's key of a backing (lit, from the
   payments' intents) is refused (`INVALID`). A window move's own target is left out: a failed move that evidence later reads
   final may sit beside the next move to it.

**Rationale:** making each retry conflict with every failed payment of its request keeps "at most one lands" without having
to prove that `failed` is terminal. Pool-v3 already has the conflict in the exact output the request names. Lit-v1's outputs
differ by inputs, so its conflict is a shared nullifier; a retry spending the failed payment's very notes even derives the same
output to the key, and only the change's key differs. Alternatives: keep the reservation (the holder asks the payee for a new
request, a second round trip, and a second request has the same double-pay risk with nothing to stop it); release it without a
conflict (pays twice in lit if the failed record lands); judge `failed` terminal (an argument about every source of failure
and every door, where the conflict needs none); reuse the failed alias (an exact retry returns the saved record by design).
**Costs:** a profile change for both wallet files (no retained state holds one); a lit retry may spend a larger note than the
smallest covering one.

**Unchanged:** a lit window move's fee request stays held by any saved payment naming it, failed or not, now also in the
move's saving turn; the move's own target already accepts a new move once one fails (Next 4 (bc)).
**Limit:** a backup's install checks distinct outputs, not that a lit retry met its failed predecessor; a backup is
authenticated by the holder's own key.

**Evidence:** `test/pool-v3-payer.test.ts` (a payment admitted but written as failed: its retry over another note pays the same
request and fee outputs and is refused `OUTPUT`; the first goes final, the retry and the payment that took its freed note fail);
`test/lit-wallet.test.ts` (a retry spends the failed payment's note though an exact one is free, goes final, the failed one is
refused `SPENT`, the payee credits the retry's output; a failed payment whose input another statement spent in canonical history
needs nothing met; refusals where no note is free and where the failed payment is final; a failed fee met; a rule-blocked
selection; a repeated statement; a window move's fee key held across concurrent calls); `test/pool-v3-wallet-backup.test.ts` (a
twin payment of one output refused). Unit suite, `check:scripts` (lit and pool command drills) and `check:docs` pass.
- Review (a fresh adversarial reviewer, of `273beda`): one major, reproduced and fixed: the saving turn's check of a window
  move's fee key read only non-failed payments, so concurrent calls let a move pay a key a failed payment also paid, both admitted
  (now strict in that turn, with a regression test). Minors taken: a rule-blocked selection refused as `FUNDS`; a repeated
  statement's refusal named no remedy; a backup holding two live payments of one output was no longer refused by the schema. Notes
  taken: the backing filter; a stale profile name in the guide and a stale comment. Found sound: no pool path lands both (exact
  outputs, failed payments never reproved, a resubmission refused `OUTPUT`), the canonical-spend rule, `selectMeeting` against
  brute force (2·10⁵ cases; 15 ms over 2·10⁴ notes), every `saved_outputs` reader with two rows of one output.
- Read-back of `9126c39`: all six closed (the major's probe now refused in the move's turn). One new minor, taken: the install
  check over every lit output key refused a backup the wallet could write and had already frozen at export (a failed move read
  final beside the next move); it now reads payees' keys only, with a test restoring that state. The move test now asserts
  that the freed note went to the other payment first.

## 2026-10-08 — Raise a restored lit wallet's exposure at each read until it names a key (Next 4 (bc))

**Status:** accepted. Implements, with changes from review, the design the area-33 audit recorded for (bc) (entry
below). No specification change: lit-v1 §8 takes `h` from the backing's finalized history, and a read from a venue view
behind the lost instance's is not that history. One limit stays, which only a later version can close (below).

**Question:** a wallet restored from its seed or a copy fixed each backing's exposure at its first read, as `h + 256`.
Where that read's venue view lagged one its lost instance acted on (a node still catching up), the exposure was too
low and was never raised, so a later request named a key the lost instance had handed out. What raises it without
filling the window after every payment?

**Decision:**
1. A backing whose exposure a restoration's first read fixed is marked (`owner_restored`, with that read's venue index
   `since`) until the wallet allocates an index of it (a request, or a payment's or burn's change). Every read, of any
   backing, raises each marked backing's exposure to `h' + 256`, where `h'` is §8's reach over the backing's outputs
   other than those of the wallet's payments prepared from a checkpoint witnessed after `since`
   (`owner_restored_saved`). A payment prepared from an older checkpoint counts.
2. The fence (M13f) compares a marked backing's outputs with the raised exposure: within it an output reads as the lost
   instance's, since a late payment to one of its requests is indistinguishable from another instance acting, for as
   long as the mark lasts (it has no time limit: a wallet that only pays without change, moves or burns keeps it);
   past it, and a held note another instance spent, still stop the wallet (`FORKED`). Before, a lagging first read
   stopped the wallet once a later read found an output above that read's exposure.
3. The marks travel in the encrypted handoff (profile `moe/wallet/keyed/3`, refusing `keyed/2` files and backups; no
   retained state holds a lit wallet); `restore --copy` clears them with the exposures, and the next read marks again.
   `restore-seed`, `restore --copy` and the guide ask for the window move, not the first read, from a caught-up view.

**Rationale:** an exposure fixed at `h + 256` is a full window, so a restored wallet names no key until `h` moves past
`h'`, which only its own output does: its window move (or a payment to its own earlier request). Leaving its own
outputs out of `h'` keeps the move from raising the exposure again, which would refill the window forever. A move is
deterministic in lit-v1 (one output to the highest exposed key, `rho` from the nullifiers, deterministic signatures),
so the restored wallet's move can be the lost instance's own, byte for byte, which that instance read final before
handing out the next keys. Only a payment prepared from a checkpoint the lost instance never saw is surely the restored
wallet's own; the first read's venue index is the latest the wallet knows to bound that instance's view. Alternatives:
excluding every record saved since the restoration (the audit's design) drops the lost instance's identical move;
excluding by index instead of commitment also drops a payer's output to the move's index; asking for a fresh first
read (the prior procedure) left even the common lag case to the holder; raising without a mark refills the window
after each payment to a new request.

**Limit:** a move prepared while the node still lags, from a checkpoint after `since` but before the lost instance's
last view, may repeat byte for byte a move that instance made and read final; it is then left out of `h'`, and the next
key named can be one the lost instance handed out. The wallet cannot tell (pool-v3 §7.2 answers an exact resubmission
with the original receipt). Any other move spends the same note and conflicts: refused as `SPENT` or final first, with
the fence stopping the wallet on the held note the other spent. So the guide asks for the move from a caught-up view, or
with its fee paid to a request taken since the restoration, which the lost instance could not have named. Levers kept:
skip the exclusion where the served package already carries the statement (closes it unless the node and the package
source both lag); a spend salt or a restoration's own output form in a later version (a lit-v2 candidate, Next 10).
**Cost:** a move prepared from a checkpoint the first read had counts, so a restoration that moves at once takes two
moves and two fees; waiting for one more checkpoint takes one.

**Evidence:**
- Three tests in `test/lit-wallet.test.ts`, over a venue view that lags and then catches up, fail on main: a restoration
  named the lost instance's index-256 key; one whose own move repeated the lost instance's named that instance's 257;
  a scoped backing's read stopped as `FORKED` (and named a stale key). The first round of this patch failed the last two.
  The first also hands the mark over an encrypted backup and pays the move's index by a payer in the move's checkpoint,
  which counts (exposure 612), while the moves' own outputs do not (the first key named is 613's).
- Review (a fresh adversarial reviewer, of the first round `492acb4`; read-back of `f0daf4d`: A, B and B2 closed, the
  limit reproduced as the only remaining path and narrowed to byte-identical moves, the cost noted): two majors, both reproduced and taken (an
  identical move left out by commitment; a read of another scoped backing raising `h` but not the exposure) and a minor
  taken (a false `FORKED` through that read); notes taken: the fence's masking lasts as long as the mark, the profile
  bump, and tests asserting the refusal's message. Found sound: every allocation goes through `allocate`, inside the
  saving transaction; the window-growth rerun and discarded kept state; copy restoration and `track`; handoff order,
  foreign keys and install checks; liveness with one backing.

## 2026-10-08 — Resume a kept lit namespace only where no output row lies past its tip, and derive lit's outputs once (audit, area 33)

**Status:** accepted. This is the rolling audit of the lit-v1 runtime: `src/lit/*.ts` and the lit paths of the state
machine, recovery checks, replay store, readers, the one wallet and `cli/wallet.ts`. No specification change.

**Question:** does the runtime judge lit-v1 §§2–7 under hostile input, keep §8's custody rules, and do so with one
mechanism per rule? Two independent reviewers (verdict side, wallet side) found no blocker, one major and six minors.
- *Major (reproduced):* a wallet restored from its seed or a copy fixes its owner keys' exposure at its first read, as
  `h + 256`. Where that read's view is older than one its lost instance acted on, the exposure is fixed too low and is
  never raised. A later request then names a key the lost instance handed out (§8: each request names its own key),
  so one payer can present another's statement as its own. The window move does not heal it. The 2026-10-08 M13g entry
  recorded it as suspected and asked for a fresh enough view; `restore-seed` did not say so.
- *Minor:* resuming a kept lit namespace at its tip did not refuse an own output row past that tip (`reader.ts`
  `resumable`), although the replay then appends from it and the row becomes a live input. The journal's reopening
  already refused it. Reached only by a damaged kept file whose §14 digest was recorded again.
- *Minor:* §2's derivation was written three times (verdict view, wallet scan, byte API); only the byte API was pinned
  by the oracle tests. Equivalent today.
- *Minor:* a closed wallet kept its owner keyring's root, and the acceptance, presenter and issue roots were not zeroed.
- *Minor:* lit's selection refusal told the holder to freshen, which lit has no use for.
- *Minor:* the test of the move guard against a burn's change passed because the window was not full.
- *Minor:* a lit fault-evidence frame longer than the reader's budget allows is refused as a resource limit even where
  a target field is over 4096 bytes (malformed under §6).

**Decision:**
1. `resumable` checks the kept namespace's own output rows with the tip check, as the journal's reopening does.
2. `createdOutputs` (records.ts) is §2's derivation; `derivedOutputs`, the view's outputs and the scan's openings go
   through it, a settlement's from its demand as the replay stood it up.
3. A closed wallet closes its keyring; each derived root is zeroed after its one secret.
4. Lit's selection names no freshen; `restore-seed` prints the freshness condition for a lit wallet, and the command
   drill asserts it; the move-guard test fills the window.
5. The fault-evidence classification is kept: a frame over the budget's whole bound is refused before it is copied,
   with the same effect as a suffix over budget, which both constructions already refuse that way; fault items are
   capped at 32 and 1 MiB.
6. The major is deferred as (bc) with its design below, since every fix found changes the custody rule's state.

**Rationale and (bc)'s design:** keeping exposure at `h + 256` on every read would refill the window after each payment
to a new request. A restored wallet always moves its window before its first request (its window is full), and its own
move is final only in a read past the restoration, which covers everything the lost instance saw. So the fix is: while a
backing's exposure is restoration-derived and the wallet has allocated no index since, each read raises it to
`h' + 256`, where `h'` is §8's reach over outputs not created by statements saved since the restoration (so its own
move does not count; a copy's statements saved before it do). This needs a restoration watermark and a per-backing
flag carried by a handoff. A payment to the move's target index by another party is the one edge to test.

**Unconfirmed and closed:** a force overlay rebuilt on a base that ended a lit demand would drop a forced settlement's
nullifiers (lit takes them from the demand). It is unreachable: a forced release needs an open gap, which lapses every
later checkpoint of its segment (C2b.4.1), and a new segment's base either imports the snapshot it was forced against or
holds it in its adopted block (C2b.4.2).

**Evidence:**
- The past-tip test fails on main (`discardKept` never called) and passes with the fix; the move-guard test now refuses
  with the guard's own message. The major's reproducers (a restored wallet reading an older view, then a fresh one,
  with and without a final move) returned the lost instance's index-257 key; they stay in the reviewer's scratch until
  (bc) ports them.
- Unit suite 1,386 passed; typecheck; lit command drill; `check:scripts`; PR CI green on its real-proof groups.
- Patch review (a fresh reviewer): one major taken. The first fix walked a namespace's own rows from leaf 0 at every
  resumed checkpoint (1,900 rows for a read of 200 outputs over 20 checkpoints). The past-tip check now runs inside
  the rebuild's one walk, and the known prefix keeps the highest row position walked; a test bounds the walk at 200
  rows and fails on the first fix. Its read-back found two minors, taken: output leaves are not unique, so a phantom
  row at leaf 0 that a lower check passed over was never walked again, past the tip or at it (the second older than
  this patch). A walk now starts again at the lowest leaf it passed over and checks the tip after the walk; a test of
  both orders fails before. Two further minors taken: a closed keyring refuses to derive a secret, and a failed wallet
  open closes its keyring. One scope note kept: kept nullifier and demand rows past a tip are vouched for by §14's
  digest alone, as pool-v3 §14 accepts; a kept demand row the trail never stood up is (bf).
- Reviewers found sound: strict decoding and keys; authorization lengths; publication (478/569), fault-evidence (244,
  4096, exact length) and trail (8200) bounds; split-but-undecodable records failing replay; per-input owner
  signatures, the presenter's withdrawal, K of the demand's own backing and the owner key on every acceptance; C3.8
  reading both signatures; widened sums, issue and burn bounds, duplicate and mixed inputs; compact intrinsic
  exclusion's exact list; every frame's length and context; §8's derivations against RFC 5869; index persistence before
  exposure; crediting (own-funded, own issues, acceptance keys, the move's output); restoration's scan; acceptance
  signing only as K over a standing demand of its backing.

**Deferred** (WORK.md Next 4): (bc) above; (bd) a payment that failed blocks paying its request again under another
alias (pool too); (be) a lit scan's predicate holds a copy of the seed for its read, never zeroed; (bf) a kept lit demand row that no trail
record stood up is never checked, and a settlement would derive its output from it (needs a re-recorded digest); drift between the
pool and keyed wallet flows (`keyOf` copies, positional intents, seven `keyed*` methods) joins Next 8.

## 2026-10-08 — Bitcoin's records reached mempool.space's mempool before mining; size a Bitcoin depth by the excluding third (slice 17)

**Status:** accepted (probe evidence; no specification change).

**Question:** probe 1 ([slice 16](#2026-10-08--keep-bitcoin-as-the-second-venue-direction-its-records-are-delayed-by-a-third-of-blocks-not-excluded-and-its-reader-exceeds-the-declared-transfer-budget-slice-16))
found that two thirds of blocks mine OP_RETURN records over 83 B. It could not tell whether those records came
through the public mempool or out of band to a pool. Out-of-band submission would leave a holder who publishes
openly to the excluding pools' policy. Probe 1 also could not say how long a record waits. That wait must fit C3.3's
window, which on Ergo is `depth + 2` blocks from authorization to inclusion
([venue-ergo §2](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/blob/46e6933/venue-ergo.md#2-index-finality-and-lag)).
The decisive part of probe 3, publishing our own records, spends real funds. The read-only part asks the same
questions of other parties' records. Its falsifiers, set in advance, were that most records are unseen by the public
mempool, or that the including pools skip seen records well beyond the control.

**Decision:**
1. Bitcoin stays the second-venue direction. Read-only probe 3 meets neither falsifier
   ([results](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md#results-of-read-only-probe-3-slice-17)).
   - Almost every record mined had reached mempool.space's mempool first, a far smaller unseen share than for ordinary
     transactions.
   - The including pools skip records from their templates somewhat more often than ordinary transactions of the
     same fee band. The largest, Foundry, skipped none.
2. The exclusion acts on records the public mempool carries. AntPool's template group skipped every one it held in
   its template, and MARA and OCEAN did so in some of their blocks. The resulting wait is heavier in its tail than slice
   16's independent-block model.
3. Input to a future `venue-bitcoin.md`, not selected here: its depth sets C3.3's margin, and its text should state
   the measured share of records mined within `depth + 2` blocks beside the depth. A shallow depth leaves a material
   share of demands to be filed again, and a depth near four leaves a few percent. venue-ergo sets no depth floor, and
   a holder judges a backing by the depth it declares.
4. The mainnet publication of our own 136 B and 15.5 KB records stays the decisive remainder (Open questions). It tests
   a fee we choose and a sender with no chain of unconfirmed parents, which other parties' records cannot. It also tests
   whether a record sent only through the public mempool reaches the including pools. "Seen by mempool.space" does not
   rule out the same record also going to a pool directly.

**Rationale:** neither falsifier holds. What remains is delay from about a third of blocks, which the depth must absorb.
That needs a declared depth, not a new mechanism. Considered and set aside:
- *Watching the mempool live with our own node:* the cloud run has no Bitcoin node, and a few hours of watching would
  see fewer records. mempool.space's block audits record the same observation, its template and first sight, for every
  block of the week. The cost is relying on one observer.
- *Waiting for funded publication before measuring:* the read-only answer bounds the delay now. Publication still owes
  the answer for our own records.

**Review:** one fresh adversarial reviewer (#157) found no blocker. Its three major findings were fixed before merge:
- "Skipped" is mempool.space's "censored" list. That list excuses low-fee, fresh and replaced transactions, so skips
  are a lower bound.
- Empty and thin blocks had been read as excluding records. They are now counted apart.
- "Seen" is narrowed to mempool.space's mempool.

The minor findings were also fixed: the model is compared like for like, the refiling shares are lower bounds, the
result hashes its input and sources, and the numbers stay in the results.

**Evidence:** [results](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md#results-of-read-only-probe-3-slice-17), with the tooling and its
JSON result at [e8b218f](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/reference-ts/tree/e8b218f/scripts/venue/bitcoin-probes).
Every raw block came from blockstream.info and was verified by probe 1's parser. The summary from mempool.space was checked
against the parsed txids. Limits (in the results):
- One observer, with its relay policy, peering, broadcast API, first-sight clock and pool attribution, under two
  template algorithms.
- Audits that change after the fact.
- Skips counted only for transactions mined in the week.
- Blocks waited read from header timestamps.
- Fee bands absolute across the week.
- Records that are other parties' transactions.

## 2026-10-08 — Keep Bitcoin as the second venue direction: its records are delayed by a third of blocks, not excluded, and its reader exceeds the declared transfer budget (slice 16)

**Status:** accepted (direction and probe evidence; no specification change).

**Question:** the [venue research](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md#4-recommendation) holds that Ergo alone is not
enough for material value: $28/h rents a majority, and one address mined 51% of its blocks. Bitcoin is the
second-venue direction ([2026-08-27](../decisions/2026-08.md#2026-08-27--venues-ergo-is-queued-bitcoin-is-the-direction-after-it)),
and the research named two cheap probes that could falsify it before any `venue-bitcoin.md`. Do major pools exclude
OP_RETURN records over 83 B (probe 1)? And what does §13.2's exhaustion cost a reader that verifies with its own
code (probe 2)? The research said probe 1 falsifies the plan if major pools exclude such records, which would leave
only the witness envelope.

**Decision:**
1. Bitcoin stays the second-venue direction. Probe 1 finds **delay, not exclusion**: the pools on AntPool's
   templates, about a third of blocks, mine no OP_RETURN over 83 B and no multi-OP_RETURN transaction, and the
   other pools, about two thirds, mine them at every size a record needs. A record therefore waits a geometric number
   of blocks for an including one ([results](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md#results-of-probes-1-and-2-slice-16)).
2. Input to a future Bitcoin profile, not selected here: carry each record in **one OP_RETURN output**, with the witness
   envelope as the fallback. Every reader pays exhaustion every day, and records in OP_RETURN let it read blocks without
   witness, which halves that cost. A single OP_RETURN per record avoids relying on multi-OP_RETURN transactions, which
   Braiins also excludes. The witness envelope is mined by every pool except OCEAN. It would buy the excluded third
   back at twice the reader's bytes, a quarter of the fee rate per byte and a commit/reveal pair of transactions.
3. A Bitcoin reader **fails the declared reader gate**. Its venue part alone exceeds the steady-state transfer budget,
   [declared](../docs/PRODUCTION_REQUIREMENTS.md#target-scale-and-budgets) before testing, by more than double.
   Venue and pool together come to the edge of the 24 h first sync. The budgets are not revised here. A
   Bitcoin-specific reader line, if one is wanted, is a separate budget decision. It would be derived from the declared
   hardware rather than from this measurement, and reviewed with this breach disclosed. Until then a Bitcoin reader
   is a desktop or server role outside the release's declared reader, and Ergo stays the venue the release measures.
4. Next is probe 3, the inclusion latency of real records against fee. Its decisive part is mainnet publication, which
   spends real funds and lies outside the standing authorization; signet shows only mechanics. Meanwhile a read-only
   variant can watch the public mempool for transactions carrying OP_RETURN over 83 B and time their first sight to
   inclusion against fee and pool, beside ordinary transactions at the same fee rate (WORK.md Next 12). The
   venue-moving record stays in Next 9, because a backing chooses its venue at creation and no record yet moves one.

**Rationale:** probe 1's falsifier was exclusion by major pools. The group that excludes is the largest pool and its
template users, but it is not a majority, and the witness carrier stays open even there. Exclusion by a third adds
waiting time, which the lag and C3.3's window must cover; it does not stop a record. Considered and set aside:
- *Litecoin as the fallback:* it is needed only if Bitcoin fails probe 1.
- *Compact block filters (BIP158) to cut the reader's bytes:* no header commits to them, so they are a supplier's
  word, which §13.2 refuses.
- *Raising the reader's budget now:* that would change a declared budget after the measurement it judges.

**Evidence:** [results](../docs/VENUE_ALTERNATIVES.md#results-of-probes-1-and-2-slice-16), with the tooling and its JSON
result at [1ddf862](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/reference-ts/tree/1ddf862/scripts/venue/bitcoin-probes). The
parser's self-test passes one real block and refuses its hostile variants by their named checks: proof of work,
height, trailing and truncated bytes, Merkle root, witness commitment and the duplicated pair. Limits (in the results):
one fortnight's policy, the supplier's pool attribution, an unanchored header chain whose evidence rests on two
suppliers agreeing, out-of-band submission as a confound, a delay model, the venue part alone against whole-reader
budgets, and sizes without witness computed from full blocks rather than fetched.

One fresh reviewer found no blocker. block.mjs matches Bitcoin Core on every check it performs, and each figure was
recomputed from the raw output. Findings and dispositions:
- *Major:* an earlier draft let a Bitcoin profile declare its own reader line, which would have been a budget set
  after the measurement. Fixed: item 3 records the failed gate, and any such line is a separate decision.
- *Overstated claims:* "meets" compared the venue part with whole-reader budgets; the chain is unanchored;
  out-of-band submission was not named; the per-day figures and the delay model were conservative without saying so.
  Fixed by stating each in the results.
- *Process:* numbers were repeated outside the results, and the tooling had not yet retired. Fixed: this entry and
  WORK.md keep summaries only, and the tooling retires, cited at 1ddf862.
- *Thin negative tests:* the reviewer confirmed the chain's median-time, bits and retarget refusals. The rest are
  listed as untested in the results; the tooling is a probe, not runtime.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-08 — Hash H through Barretenberg, let the journal's own read skip the proofs it verified, and slim a wallet's read notes (slice 15)

**Status:** accepted (no specification change: no byte, identity or verdict changes).

**Question:** M13h left the design point's only two budgets at their edge (WORK.md Next 4, [probes](../docs/POOL_DEPLOYMENT_PROBES.md#the-operator-apart-and-the-wallet-at-depth-m13h)):
- (ay) a statement that arrives while `serve` reads its own newly held checkpoint waits for that read, 1.30–1.48 s at
  40–44 statements a checkpoint and about 1 s at the design point's 28, against "admission ≤ 1 s per statement at
  peak" ([budgets](../docs/PRODUCTION_REQUIREMENTS.md#target-scale-and-budgets)). The read verifies the checkpoint's
  proofs again and replays them on the note tree, where JavaScript Poseidon2 took most of the main thread, inside
  the journal's one turn;
- (az) a wallet's `sync` held about 6 KB a holding, so 1 GiB near 1.2·10⁵ unspent notes, against "≤ 1 GiB,
  independent of history length".

**Decision:**
1. The host computes H (pool-v2 §1) by Barretenberg 5.2.0's own `poseidon2Hash` and `poseidon2Permutation`, on a
   single-threaded WebAssembly `BarretenbergSync` started when `poseidon2.ts` loads, from the binary the installed bb.js
   ships (named by path, so `BB_WASM_PATH` cannot replace it). The callers keep a synchronous API. The bigint
   implementation becomes a test oracle that every hash and permutation is compared with, beside the pinned vectors.
   This takes the lever the [2026-10-01 entry](#2026-10-01--verify-a-trails-proofs-ahead-of-its-replay-on-a-pool-of-verifier-instances) left untaken.
2. The journal remembers, in this process only and for at most 4,096 proofs, each proof its admission verified true,
   keyed by SHA-256 over an injective frame of kind, public inputs and proof bytes. Its own reads ask the verifier only
   for a proof not remembered; `audit` verifies every proof again.
3. A wallet's read keeps each held note as an instance of one class with prototype accessors (no closures per note),
   reads what saved records reserve and which saved demands present its notes in one query each, not two a note, and
   field elements and identifier limbs convert through one hexadecimal string, not a bigint per byte (about 2 KB of
   garbage a conversion), reading an own copy at the view's real length.

**Rationale:**
- *Reading off the admission queue* would remove the wait whatever the read costs, but admissions would then judge the
  service clock against an older read than the venue view, across `ready`, `serviceClock` and the reads file's single
  handle. The two levers above change no judgment, only work, and bring the wait well under the budget.
- *Each lever alone* falls short. Measured on one host, with checkpoints of about 42 statements:
  - the memory alone left the slow admissions at a 1.03 s median, since the replay is the larger part;
  - Barretenberg alone brought them to 0.75 s, the twice-verified proofs then the larger part.
  
  Both together brought them to about 0.42 s.
- *Other ways to H:*
  - a native bb backend is faster (0.044 ms against 0.085 ms a hash) but is a per-platform binary;
  - a faster bigint implementation would gain at most about twofold, in hand-written field arithmetic;
  - keeping the bigint hash as a runtime fallback would mean two host implementations of one hash.
- *A persisted memory* (trusting the journal's rows) would also spare the first read after a restart, but would make a
  stored row the reason a proof goes unverified. The process's own memory holds only what it verified.
- *Streaming a wallet's view* (holdings kept as rows, the output written as it is read) would make a sync's memory
  independent of its holdings. But it changes the `sync` output's construction, and every acting command still selects
  from its notes. Allocation profiles found most of the cost in how each note was read: a bigint per byte, four
  closures and accessor pairs, and two prepared statements per note. Removing those cut a wallet's memory beyond a
  reader's from about 4.0 to 2.2 KB a holding at 10⁵ holdings. That moves a seed-restored first sync's 1 GiB edge
  from about 1.3·10⁵ to 1.8·10⁵ unspent notes, and later syncs' to about 3.4·10⁵: far more than a design-point holder
  holds. It does not make the memory independent of holdings; streaming stays the lever (WORK.md Next 4 (az)).
- *Invariants:*
  - H is unchanged: the pinned vectors, the oracle over random and boundary inputs, and real proofs over
    host-computed commitments all agree;
  - a remembered verdict is the verifier's own deterministic verdict on identical bytes (the premise of
    verify-ahead.ts);
  - kept state keeps the same verifier identities;
  - field encodings accept and refuse exactly as before.
- *Costs:*
  - every process that loads the pool's primitives (each `moe` command) starts one bb.js instance, about 0.2–0.25 s
    and 30–60 MB;
  - `poseidon2.ts` is Node-only (the package declares no browser target);
  - the memory holds at most 4,096 keys, under 1 MB.

**Evidence:** [probe section](../docs/POOL_DEPLOYMENT_PROBES.md#the-design-points-two-edges-slice-15). Tests:
- the oracle comparison and the `BB_WASM_PATH` test (pool-poseidon2.test.ts);
- the memory's edges (`rememberedProofs`) and the journal's verification counts, an audit included (pool-v3-store.test.ts);
- the field conversions against the per-byte forms, with detached and lying views (pool-poseidon2.test.ts).

Review: one fresh reviewer each for (ay) and (az).
- (ay): no blocker. One major, fixed with a regression test: the hash instance ran whatever binary `BB_WASM_PATH` named,
  which the verifier refuses and which this instance, started once at import, would keep. Two minors were fixed: an
  untested audit claim and a stale root comment. The reviewer read the major's fix back.
- (az): no blocker or major. Two items were fixed: the batched queries read the wallet's whole saved history (now its
  read notes only), and a Uint8Array subclass lying about its length could change a conversion (now an own copy).

**Limits:**
- At 10⁵ the waits during the journal's own read were 0.52–0.61 s, at 42 statements a checkpoint. Whether they stay
  under 1 s at 10⁶ depends on how they grow with depth, which only the 10⁶ run settles.
- After a restart, the first admission once the previous process's last commitment is held reads what that process
  admitted, which this one does not remember: 1.0–1.5 s at 42 statements a checkpoint (about 0.7–1.0 s at 28).
  - A read before `serve` listens was tried and withdrawn. That commitment is held only later, so the read gained
    nothing, and at the restart's index it discarded the kept reads (WORK.md Next 4 (bb)).
  - Remembering proofs across processes would need the rows as the reason, which point 2 declines.
- A wallet's memory still grows with its holdings, about 2.2 KB each (above).
- (ba) is not measured: a lit read re-checks each marked note by design, about 14 s a read at 10⁵ marked notes, within
