  the steady-state budget at ten reads a day.
- One host and one run per measurement.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-08 — Close the code review of slices 12–14: a fenced backup's restoration spacing, a settlement to the holder's own owner, and a release check bound to its record

**Status:** accepted (review of `582d6fc..f4cf083`; no specification change).

**Question:** five fresh reviewers read the code shipped since the previous code review, by area: the wallet (M13e,
M13f, lit notes in the one wallet), lit (lit-v1's bytes, §§6 and 8, the acceptance owner), the readers and the one
evidence chain (area 31, M13b, M13g), the operator journal and transport (M12a–c, M13d, area 30), and the release
and evidence tooling (M13a, M13c, CI's real-proof gate). Each slice had had its own review. They found three material
defects and six smaller ones:
- *Journal (C2.4.1, M13d).* Recording a restoration kept the spacing a fence row already held, so a backup taken while
  the fence stood (a nightly backup during a silence wait of days, a VM snapshot) carried it. Each restoration of that
  backup signed a byte-identical return (Ed25519 is deterministic); neither saw the other's as foreign, both adopted,
  and both co-signed different statements at one position of one segment: M13d's own falsifier. The guide's "the step
  is random per restoration" did not hold.
- *Wallet (holder authorization, C3.8).* A pool-v3 holder's `settle` checked only that the acceptance verifies under the
  obligor and names a field owner. A backer who issued to the holder's request knows that request's owner and could
  accept naming it: the settlement released no claims to the backer, the demand read settled, and the holder's seed
  never found the output (only a backer's derivation finds settlement outputs). M14g5 closed the same escape for lit
  by the owner's signature and left pool-v3 to a holder that declines to release; the reference wallet never declined.
- *Release (M13a).* `--verify` compared an install only with the install lock in its own directory, so any tarball and
  lock that named each other passed, a lock with no runtime entries included. A record's `clean` read `git status`,
  which ignores `dist`: a record could name commit Y with X's build, and `--compare` accepted unclean records.
- Smaller: the package reader refused CONTEXT on the header of the segment a selected backing's own snapshot named
  where the directory's first snapshot was withheld (pool-v3 §7.1: unresolved); a lit window move paid a fee request
  an unsaved statement of its seed had already paid (M13f item 3 covered `prepare` only); lit's `foundIndices` copied
  a backing's index array per note (O(N²): 15.6 s a read at 4·10⁴ notes); `sync` left a lit wallet's presented notes
  out of `available` though lit spends them; several drill refusals checked only the exit class; the live command
  drill's report, cited as current, predates slice 13 (117 of its 153 bound sources changed).

**Decision:**
1. Recording a restoration while its return is unsigned draws a fresh spacing and keeps its index. Nothing is signed
   under a spacing before the return, which records the opening in its own transaction, so a changed spacing never
   leaves two returns of one instance. Two restorations of a fenced backup now sign returns at different sequences:
   the lower publishes nothing over the higher and adopts nothing (`CONFLICT`), as for two restorations of one backup.
2. The pool-v3 holder's `settle` refuses `OWN_KEY`, before any proof, where the acceptance names the owner of a note
   it holds, of an output of its own statements, or of a receive request it made or recovered (the keys derived once
   for all requests). The holder keeps its notes, visible and demandable again, instead of releasing them to an output
   its seed never finds. No byte, identity or verdict changes: the wallet declines to release. In pool-v3 the declined
   demand reads as lapsed, so the backer still escapes dishonour, as M14g5's observation records; a v4 fix proves the
   owner secret in the acceptance. A wallet restored from its seed alone knows only the owners of what it holds.
3. `release.mjs --verify <directory> <record>` requires the record to name the checkout's commit from a clean tree and
   the checkout's lockfile, the directory's install lock to be byte for byte the one `installLock` derives from that
   lockfile for the record's tarball (with the record's SHA-256), and the tarball to have the record's SHA-256 and
   integrity. A record is `clean` only when the packed `dist` equals a fresh `tsc` build of the tree (source maps read
   from where they lie); `--compare` refuses unclean records.
4. Where the first snapshot is not held, a context failure of the segment the selected backing's own snapshot names
   is `unresolved-evidence`; a receipt final before such a checkpoint then reads unresolved until the first snapshot
   is supplied (withheld evidence).
5. A window move applies `paidByOwn` to its fee request; `foundIndices` appends; a lit `sync`'s `available` includes
   presented notes (`presented` stays the part a demand presented); the drills' usage checks name their refusal; the
   live command report is cited at `de1f688`, its re-run owed to the local machine.
6. Recorded as limits, not fixed: a lit key both instances hand out (each allocating past the same highest index)
   trips no fence, since keys are handed out privately (M13f's "caught by the key check" holds only past every index
   this wallet exposed); after (5), a lit read still hashes every marked note (about 0.14 ms each on a 2.1 GHz Xeon,
   about 14 s a read at 10⁵), which slice 15's (az) measures; `main` carries no branch protection, so CI binds merges
   by practice (M13a); a same-repository branch could upload a forged `real-proof-tree` artifact (accepted there).

**Alternatives:** for (1), a spacing derived from the restoring machine (no machine identity is kept) or refusing a
copy of a fenced directory (the fence row cannot tell a copy from a reopen); for (2), a pool-v4 owner signature as
lit-v1 has (a byte change; recorded for v4 by M14g5), or refusing only a request's owner (the change outputs and
held notes are as much the holder's); for (4), reading the selected backing's terms without that header (it is the
context the judgment's receipts and the walk read, so a foreign one cannot stand in).

**Evidence:** reproducers per finding under the review's scratch, each now a regression test that fails before its
fix: `test/pool-v3-recovery-store.test.ts` (a fenced backup restored beside its first restoration),
`test/pool-v3-redemption.test.ts` (`OWN_KEY` for a demanded note's and an open request's owner),
`test/release.test.ts` (four refusals, the dropped optional entry), `test/pool-v3-scope-reader.test.ts` (three reads,
one per verdict, unresolved once the first snapshot is withheld), `test/lit-wallet.test.ts` (the move's fee), and the
lit drill's `available`. `npm test` (88 files, 1,378 tests), `check:scripts`, and CI runs 37762297600 and 37764918177 (every real-proof
group on Linux and Windows, `reproducible-release` with clean records) pass.

**Review:** a fresh reviewer read the integrated patch (`f4cf083..4eecf90`), its intent and CI run 37762297600; no
blocker or major. Four minor findings, all resolved: `--verify` binds only to a record anyone could write (RELEASE.md
now says the record must come from a trusted source, CI's artifact or the verifier's own `--compare`; rebuilding the
tarball in `--verify` was left out, as reproduction is `--compare`'s job); M13d item 3 and the `restored` JSDoc still
said a recording changes nothing (corrected); `ownsOwner` re-derived the master keys and a capsule per request, about
1.6 ms each (`requestOwners` derives them once); a seed-restored wallet's narrower cover (recorded in item 2). It
confirmed that nothing is signed or exposed under a spacing before the return's transaction, that a CONTEXT verdict
with the first snapshot held is unchanged, that exact retries answer before the new refusals, that a holder that is also
the backer is not refused, and that `freshBuild` compares both file lists and contents.

## 2026-10-08 — Judge lapse before the reader's own snapshot, serve every fork's trail, and disposition the rest of Next 4 (slice 13 M13g)

**Status:** accepted; review below.
**Spec change:** [venue-ergo §1 at 6cfe79b](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/commit/6cfe79b) drops a retired example (no byte, identity or verdict change). The reader changes need none: they bring the runtime to C2.10.11's order and pool-v3 §§7, 7.1.

**Question:** slice 13 (d) asks that every open Next 4 finding be fixed or dispositioned before the external review.
The release-assurance gate asks for a "documented disposition of every material finding". After M13b and M13f, 31 letters
stayed open. The 2026-10-03 direction named (o), (r) and the M8a minor for slice 13 at the latest. Which ones change a
verdict, what is served, or what a caller can rely on, and what does each of the others wait for?

**Decision:** fixed:
1. *(ah) Lapse is judged before the reader's own snapshot is read.* C2.10.11: "Term lapse reads only the authenticated
   header and scope." `judge` read the selected backing's snapshot first, so a lapsed checkpoint whose own preimage was
   withheld read unresolved. It now authenticates the header and scope from the directory's first snapshot (pool-v3
   §7.1), judges lapse, and only then reads its own snapshot. A lapsed or excluded class keeps the first snapshot in
   its row. A valid class keeps its own snapshot. `recheck` already reads a row snapshot of any directory entry.
2. *(ai) A committed snapshot that does not decode is unresolved.* A preimage that hashes to its signed digest but
   fails §7's strict codec escaped as `EncodingError`. pool-v3 §7: "A strict header codec is not a classifier of
   malformed committed evidence and its rejection alone supplies no exclusion verdict". So the reader now refuses
   `unresolved-evidence`, as for an undecodable directory (§12). Excluding the checkpoint was rejected: no exclusion
   rule names a codec rejection, and the first snapshot fixes the scope that every class reads.
3. *(r) TERMS_SCOPE is judged after a continuation's silence lapse.* C2.10.11 excludes "a scope naming a term not yet in
   force at its index or one the replacement chain does not hold" only where the checkpoint is not lapsed. A
   continuation's clock reads each scoped backing's terms, which its name commits, so the clock is the same whichever
   link the scope names. An opening has no silence lapse, so its order is unchanged. CONTEXT, TERMS_CONTEXT and
   SILENCE_SCOPE stay before any clock read, because each makes the scope's clock undefined. C2b.6.1: a header whose
   scope mixes durations "is malformed, so a scope has one interval as it has one clock". A header of another
   configuration, venue or operator, or one whose opening sequence follows the checkpoint, has no opening to run a
   clock from.
4. *(ag), (p) The package reader takes the selection's scope from the directory's first snapshot*, as `judge` does. A
   selected backing whose own snapshot names another segment therefore reads the SNAPSHOT exclusion every backing
   reads, instead of unresolved. A directory entry the scope does not name still refuses unresolved, because no
   terms read it. The read's context needs only the selected backing's terms, which its name binds, so where the first
   snapshot is not held the selected backing's own snapshot supplies them. A read that judges the selection still
   refuses without the first snapshot, and a receipt final before the selection does not need it. The Ergo replay's
   receipt group showed that need.
5. *(o) A journal serves a trail for each fork its snapshots name.* `parts()` kept one trail top per segment, the
   longest. A taken predecessor whose snapshots lie on two forks was therefore served only the longer trail, and a
   reader of the shorter one stayed unresolved. The journal now keeps, per segment, the tops no other reaches: one in
   the common case, and one cost per snapshot in range, a `reaches` that is a row lookup unless a fork is kept at that
   position. *(as), trail half:* one `topTrails` serves the tops for the journal and the replica, using the replica's
   rule: furthest first, and none that a served trail or the reader's base reaches. The object half stays two loops
   over two sources: the journal's signed and taken rows, and the replica's index.
6. *(at)* `VenueError` carries an optional code. The Ergo publisher sets `TOO_LARGE` and `FULL`, and `relayRefusal`
   reads the code, not the message. *(m)* An `evidence.db` of another layout refuses `STORAGE`. *(ab)* On an alias
   already saved, `accept`'s and `demand`'s `--deadline +n` reads as the saved deadline, so the same command line
   rerun after the index moved is the exact retry. An absolute deadline is compared as given, and another demand or
   quantity still conflicts. *(ac)* `keepContext` closes the in-memory rows of the views it builds.
7. *(ae)* venue-ergo §1's example of unreadable records named the retired transparent path, beside lit-v1, which the
   profile reads. The example is dropped.

Dispositioned, each with what would reopen it:
- *Closed by earlier work:* the M8a minor. `requireConfigurationVerifier` refuses a verifier without the configuration's
  declared identities (slice 9).
- *(v) Unreachable through the commands.* Every `moe` command takes an exclusive lock on its directory's `lock.db`
  before it opens any file, so no second process commits between a kept file's opening check and its connection. A
  library caller opening one kept file from two processes stays outside, as the storage decision states. It reopens
  if a command ever opens another directory's kept file.
- *Accepted limits:* (n) a spent note's witness stays kept. lit-v1 §8's highest index reads spent witnesses, and each
  witness's rewrites are bounded. (an) a remade lit burn takes a new change index. (af) a journal file with a deleted
  nullifier row refuses at the next insert with an uncoded error and signs nothing. This is storage damage inside the
  journal's own file, under the open durable-storage qualification gate.
- *Performance only, taken when a measured budget fails:* (j) verify-only key bytes; (t) a settle or presentation read
  decoding every acceptance and release; (u) re-verifying kept replacements; (aj) the non-service count rereading
  every publication (with M4's hostile-evidence growth, Next 9); (ak) a sibling's read replaying again; (al) the keyed
  scan's identity naming every held backing's window; (m)'s sync abort signal (each exchange already has a 10 s idle
  bound).
- *Replica availability, taken when the replica is next changed:* (ap) a terms field learned later is never served
  past a mark; (aq) a hostile upstream's objects are indexed (an extension of M4's storage limit); (ar) a backing whose
  canonical sequence lags another's is never kept.
- *At the change that needs it:* (y) carry `seen` in the handoff at the next wallet profile change. Until then it
  costs refusals, never funds. (ad) a mainnet anchor's difficulty floor, with the slice that enables mainnet: the CLI
  refuses mainnet. (z) reporting a venue-created output awaiting adoption (pool-delivery C4.7), when a holder path
  exercises force-created outputs. It is never reported as spendable. (ao) a reader's kept fallback when no source
  answers is beyond the smallest profile. A replica serves that case.
- *Tooling, with its next change:* (g) the journal crash drill arms only the commit boundary of open, submit and
  commit. SQLite rolls back an uncommitted transaction whole. (aa) a testnet drill sweep mode, with the next live
  testnet run on the local machine.

**Rationale:** C2.10.11 fixes the order in which classes are judged, and pool-v3 §7 says what a decoder's rejection
means. Items 1–4 bring the reader to that text without changing any byte. A reader that previously excluded a
TERMS_SCOPE continuation now lapses it where a gap is open, and leaves it unresolved where its clock is. A kept walk
whose stored class differs is discarded and classified again (§14). Item 5 is availability. Serving more trails adds
bytes only where a fork exists, and a reader's take keeps whichever trails it already holds. The dispositions follow
AGENTS.md: a performance lever is taken when a budget needs it, never speculatively.

**Evidence:** at `d489375`:
- Four scope-reader tests and one journal serve test fail on main and pass with the fixes.
- A CLI test asserts the STORAGE code.
- The relay and publisher tests assert the codes.
- The lit command drill reruns `demand` and `accept` with `+n` after the index moved and gets the saved deadline.

At `d489375`, the unit suite (1,368 tests), `check:scripts` and `check:pool:local-replay` (223 groups, 65 real proofs, on
a 2.1 GHz Xeon) passed. On the final code after the review fixes they passed again: the unit suite (1,371 tests), `check:scripts`, and
the local replay (223 groups, 65 real proofs, with the alien-header group revised as recorded below), and the replay
with the Ergo adapter (230 groups, 65 real proofs).

**Review:** an independent adversarial review of `1fcf637..d489375` found no blocker, and nothing that turns a valid
class into another or the reverse. It found:
- *Fixed* (`6ab2405`), each with a test that fails before the fix:
  - F1: (ai) was incomplete. A receipt walk's reference check still let an undecodable first snapshot escape as
    `EncodingError`, which the CLI reads as `INVALID`. It is now unresolved.
  - F2: the package reader demanded the selection's full trail before the walk. A term-lapsed selection served
    §12's count-zero trail therefore read unresolved, while the walk lapsed it. The trail is now the judgment's to
    demand: `judge` requires it for a valid class and before an exclusion. The
    eager demand also kept the selected envelope complete (protocol rules, §9.1 row): a compact fault never stands in
    for the selection's own trail. The local replay's import group and `pool-v3-frontier`'s selected-envelope test
    showed this, and `judge` now keeps that rule itself.
    The selected checkpoint never takes §9.1's compact exclusion, while its lapse still needs no trail.
  - F5: CONTEXT's operator and opening-sequence part was judged before term lapse, which reads only the header,
    scope and chain. It is now judged after term lapse and before any clock read. Domain and venue stay first,
    because their chain is not on this record.
  - F7: a stale comment.
- *Recorded:*
  - F3: a selected backing that the first snapshot's scope does not name still refuses unresolved, as it did before
    M13g. No terms of that checkpoint read it, and the walk classifies it SCOPE for every descent. Reading it would
    take terms from another segment's head.
  - F4: moving TERMS_SCOPE costs availability. A continuation whose scope names a foreign backing under a link not
    in force now reads that backing's clock. A reader then needs that backing's evidence before the checkpoint's
    index, or the read is unresolved. This is C2.10.11's text (lapse first, and the clock reads every scoped
    backing). Only a dishonest operator triggers it, and such an operator can already freeze its backing by
    withholding.
  - F6: the fault pass on a refused read (`inspectRefused`) authenticates scope from the backing's own snapshot. It
    is observational only, and unchanged from main.
  - F7: no test checks that the publisher sets `FULL`. An opening that fails both TERMS_SCOPE and SILENCE_SCOPE is
    now labelled SILENCE_SCOPE.
- *Checked and sound:*
  - every valid return passes the reader's own snapshot and the sibling check;
  - lapsed and excluded rows' first snapshot is never read as the backing's own;
  - kept classes from older code are discarded, because the kept context hashes the code;
  - clock state stays a function of backing and opening index;
  - `topTrails` keeps an antichain at O(1) rows a snapshot;
  - (ab) still refuses another demand or quantity.

A read-back of `6ab2405` was clean:
- no path relied on the package reader's eager trail;
- a valid class still needs its trail, and an exclusion needs the trail unless §9.1's compact certificate replaces
  it;
- a header of another operator, or with a later opening sequence, reaches no clock or opening code.

Its test nits are applied: two test titles, and the F5 test now covers a header of another operator too. With F5,
the local replay's alien-header group (`scope-check.mjs`) reads differently. Its checkpoint signed after x's term
ended was unresolved, because its header-context fault was judged before lapse and its records were withheld. It now
lapses, as the late checkpoint beside it does. The group keeps its original assertion at a live index: a provable
header-context fault cannot exclude without the trail.

## 2026-10-08 — Build a claim-layer profile only where it serves a function no other profile serves

**Status:** direction. It narrows the [2026-10-05 direction](#2026-10-05--build-extensions-claim-layer-profiles-beside-the-shielded-pool-each-chosen-per-backing)
(build Extensions' profiles beside the pool), whose order it keeps; AGENTS.md's direction paragraph states it.

**Question:** the 2026-10-05 direction builds every Extensions profile beside the pool, cheapest first, because the
specification prices each one and a deployment can choose. It does not say when a profile is not worth building.
A profile that another built profile matches or beats on every property a deployment could choose it for adds code
to review and secure, a fault surface and a wallet path, and offers holders only a worse choice.

**Decision:**
1. A claim-layer profile is built only where it serves a function no other built profile serves. One that is
   strictly worse than another (no property it gives that the other does not) is not built. Each built profile's
   documentation states its tradeoffs against the others, and the choice stays per backing in E.
2. *The shielded pool is the default* for a new backing: private payments, public supply verification and
   resistance to refusal aimed at one holder.
3. *Lit notes (`moe/lit/v1`) serve links that carry only tiny packets* (a mesh network, a low-rate radio), where a
   pool statement's proof (about 14.7 KB) cannot travel, and statements a party checks without a prover. Their price
   is Extensions' (every holding and transfer public and linked). So lit is developed for that function: the next lit
   work measures its frames (publications up to 569 bytes, requests, acceptances) against such a link's packet budget,
   and any size reduction that changes bytes is a lit-v2 candidate under the decision rule; lit-v1 is fixed by its name.
4. *Offline payment serves payment where neither party reaches a sequencer at all.* Its stated price: it identifies
   a double-spender after the fact rather than preventing the double spend (the residual runs to deposit against
   the payer's key), and it needs the payer's identity in the note, so it does not work for an anonymous payer. It
   stays after release.
5. *The accumulator and Chaumian profiles* are designed only after a comparison shows a function that neither the
   pool, lit nor offline payment serves. Chaumian gives up public supply verification (supply becomes the signer's
   assertion), so it must show what it buys for that.

**Rationale:** fewer mechanisms at equal capability is the project's standing preference (AGENTS.md), and a strictly
dominated profile is a mechanism with no capability of its own. Keeping the choice per backing with stated
tradeoffs preserves open entry: a backer chooses, the reference does not hide costs. Alternatives: build every
profile Extensions prices (the 2026-10-05 reading: more choice, but including choices no backer should make); the
pool only (fewer mechanisms, but no option for links a proof cannot cross or for payment without any sequencer).

**Limits:** "strictly worse" is judged against the built profiles at the time; a later profile or a measured link
budget can change it. Lit's function rests on a packet budget not yet measured against its frames.

**Spec change:** none: Construction declares the claim layer per backing and Extensions prices each profile; which
profiles the reference builds is the reference's choice.

## 2026-10-07 — Bind a settlement's output to its acceptance, fence a wallet that sees another instance of its seed act, and refuse a lit request the seed already paid (slice 13 M13f)

**Status:** accepted after design and integrated review.
**Spec change:** [pool-recovery C3.5 and its appendix at 114799e](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/commit/114799e).
No pool-v3 byte, identity or verdict changes: C3.5 already makes the derivation's bytes the wallet's own, and no reader,
journal or conformance object reads it.

**Question:** slice 13 (d) asks that Next 4's open correctness findings be fixed or dispositioned. M13e item 6 left the
wallet's three items of the durable-operation gate ("restored backups and obsolete instances cannot cause conflicting
exposed signatures or silently lose accepted operations"):
- (av) no fence against an obsolete instance beside its restored copy, or a seed restoration beside a live wallet;
- (aw) lit: a restored wallet cannot tell a request its lost instance paid, or has in flight (M13e F3);
- (ax) pool-v3: a restored wallet may settle at the disclosure count of a release its lost instance has unwitnessed (F6).

Reading (ax) found a defect without any restoration. C3.5 derived `rho_out` "from its root secret, the settlement's input
nullifiers, the segment identity and a disclosure count: the number of releases of this demand bound to this segment".
The count is per demand but the derivation read no demand, and the wallet presents an earlier demand's notes again by
design (M10b item 9, `demandSelection`, with deterministic padding). So a second demand over the same notes in the same
segment settled at count 0 to the output the first demand's release had disclosed, which K could insert first for any
owner of its own acceptance: what the count exists to stop. A regression test reproduced it on main (equal `rho_out`).

**Decision:**
1. *`rho_out` reads the acceptance identity* (C3.5 corrected): `settlementRho(seed, domain, nfs, segment, acceptance, count)`.
   The acceptance names its demand, so two demands over the same notes never share an output; two acceptances of one
   demand at one count do not either, so a release discloses only its own acceptance's output. This also closes F6: a
   restored wallet settling under any acceptance but the lost instance's names an output K cannot compute; settling under
   the lost instance's acceptance reproduces the lost settlement (the same act). What stays: K, holding an unwitnessed
   release, presents its acceptance again and inserts that output first; the settle is then refused at the wallet's
   output check before it discloses anything, as for a single wallet whose release K withheld, and the remedy is the same
   (withdraw and demand again). The same-count guard `pendingSettlement`, whose premise was "rho_out reads no acceptance",
   is removed. Wallet profile `moe/wallet/v3/10`; no retained state holds a moe wallet.
2. *A fence from public evidence (av).* Each read keeps the backing's held notes (`held_notes`, `held_read`, never
   exported). Evidence that another instance of the seed acted, which only a holder of the seed can produce:
   - a note held at the previous read of the backing is spent (canonical state with force) by a statement this wallet did
     not save: the statement that inserted the nullifier is compared with the saved records and the ones a reproof
     superseded (a forced spend, with the wallet's saved settlements among the forced publications), so a saved record
     over the same note that failed is no cover, and another instance's settlement of a shared demand counts;
   - lit: a read finds an own key of a backing paid at an index above every index this wallet exposed and found, where the
     exposure is known (a wallet persists an index before exposing its key, and a payer pays only a key it was given).
   The evidence and the held set commit in one transaction (`wallet_fork`). Every acting operation and a new handoff then
   refuses `FORKED`, naming it; `supply`, `sync` (which reports it), `presentation`, `submit`/`publish` of saved bytes and an
   exact retry of a saved alias (which answers with its saved record) stay open, since they sign nothing new. `recordRestoration` (`moe wallet restore --copy`), the owner's statement that
   this is now the only instance, clears it, and the next read is the baseline. A view older than the backing's last read,
   or one with no canonical checkpoint, changes nothing. A new wallet, a seed restoration and a handoff destination start
   with no held set, so their first read trips nothing.
3. *A lit payment refuses a request its seed already paid (aw):* where a statement spending this seed's notes, which
   this wallet did not save, created the request's exact output (`CONFLICT`). The replay keeps every own mark, spent
   ones included, the statements that spent each tag and the nullifiers each consumed; a lit output derives from those
   nullifiers in input order and its position (lit-v1 §2), and a spend consumes one or two notes into at most four
   outputs, so each such statement gives at most eight candidates. The cost follows the wallet's own spends.

**Rationale and alternatives:**
- For (ax): the demand identity alone closes the cross-demand case but not F6. A policy refusing a restored wallet's
  settlement of a demand it did not save (proposed first, with withdraw and demand again as the remedy) closes F6 too, but
  takes C3.5's seed re-proof away and costs a withdrawal and a demand proof on every restoration rather than only when K
  attacks; the design review showed the acceptance binding needs neither. A later version binding `rho_out` in the
  relation is unnecessary for the same reason. Counting releases per note set instead of per demand changes the count
  readers read.
- For (av): no local mechanism prevents two holders of a seed from acting, and the venue's nullifier rule keeps money
  consistent; the harms are lit key reuse across live instances, conflicting settlements and silent divergence. A
  coordination record or an online lease would be a coordination authority (AGENTS.md). Detection, a named refusal and
  the owner's decision cost one small table per backing.
- For (aw): a key index over every canonical output (about 40 bytes per output) or a scan of history per payment were the
  alternatives considered first; the review pointed to the replay's own rows, which make the complete check for
  witnessed payments cost what the wallet itself spent.
- Costs: one rewrite of the held notes' nullifiers per read and one lookup per held note that disappeared; per lit
  payment, a pass over the wallet's own marks and up to eight hashes per unsaved own spend.

**Evidence:** stand-in proofs and reference fixtures.
- `test/pool-v3-redemption.test.ts`: two demands over the same notes in one segment, the first's release witnessed
  without force, settle to distinct `rho_out` (equal on main); a second acceptance at one count names its own output, and
  the derivation is checked in every settle test.
- `test/pool-v3-wallet-backup.test.ts`: a seed restoration beside a live wallet pays from a note the live wallet holds;
  the live wallet's sync reports the evidence, resolves its records, refuses request, prepare, burn, fulfill and export
  `FORKED` without proving, still submits saved bytes and keeps the evidence across reopening; `recordRestoration`
  clears it, and the other instance trips on the live one's next spend. `test/pool-v3-wallet.test.ts`: a forced
  settlement of a received note by a key the wallet does not hold trips it, and `SPENT` is reached after a restoration.
- `test/lit-wallet.test.ts`: a copy and a seed restoration refuse paying again an invoice the lost instance paid; a
  request nobody paid passes; a second instance's window move trips the live wallet by its key above `exposed`, and the
  live wallet's move trips the second; where both instances spend one note and the live wallet's own record fails, the
  other's spend trips it and the other's request is refused as paid. `scripts/pool/v3/wallet-crash.mjs` keeps its
  twenty-seven exits (the operation's own read writes nothing once the setup read is its baseline), and the lit command
  drill shows the handoff's wallet refusing `FORKED` after the seed restoration's window move, until `restore --copy`.
- Not covered: real proofs (the same code paths), a statement of the lost instance still in flight at the restoration
  (it trips the fence when it lands; a retry the copy made first is a duplicate payment the payee holds), a note another
  instance received and spent between two reads of this one (never held here; lit's window case is caught by the key
  check only past every index this wallet exposed: a key both instances hand out is not, review-code 2026-10-08), and a copy restored without `restore --copy` (identity can miss it; the fence then trips on the first spend).

**Review:** a fresh reviewer read the proposal, the specification and the code before implementation:
- confirmed the cross-demand collision and that a demand-bound derivation is minimal for it;
- *medium:* (aw)'s "only complete check" was false: the replay's `witness`, `nullifier` and `consumedAt` rows give a check
  costing the wallet's own spends. Taken: item 3;
- *medium–low:* the acceptance binding was dismissed on a false premise (it changes no public byte) and closes F6 without
  refusing seed re-proof. Taken: item 1, replacing the refusal policy;
- *low–medium:* editing a contract pool-v3 instantiates needs the argument that this is a correction (no byte, identity
  or verdict), made above and in the specification's appendix;
- *low–medium:* the fence can trip on a wallet's own lost history (a stale first read after a restoration, a lost
  statement landing after the baseline); recorded as intended stops in the guide;
- *low:* allow exact resubmission and publication under the fence. Taken; held-set replacement only from a newer view
  with a canonical checkpoint, committed with the evidence. Taken;
- *info:* retained reports bind `redemption.ts` and `wallet-store.ts`.
A fresh reviewer then reviewed the integrated patch (b1c8473..144b90d and the specification) and found every acting path
fenced, the observe paths signing nothing, the derivation sound and `paidByOwn`'s candidates correct. Findings, all taken:
- *medium–high:* a saved spend that failed covered the note it named, so another instance's spend of it tripped neither
  the fence nor `paidByOwn` (reproduced: both instances spend one note; the live wallet kept acting and paid the other's
  request again). Both now compare the statement that spent the note;
- *low:* an exact retry of a saved alias was refused `FORKED`; it now answers with the saved record;
- *low:* the specification's appendix now says the correction applies to pool-v3's wallets and leaves every reader's
  verdict as it was (114799e);
- *minor:* a test asserted a shared code only; it asserts the message now.
The reviewer read the fixes back (8837e95, 114799e): the reproducer now records the evidence, and reproofs, imported
namespaces and forced settlements are covered. Of two low remainders, a pool held note's tag is now derived from its
nullifier rather than read from its kept mark (a damaged mark would have stopped the wallet falsely); and `reprove`,
`keyedResign` and `fulfill` still refuse `FORKED` before reading their saved state, accepted: they sign nothing new
either way, and `payment`, `act` and `fulfillment` read the saved state.

**Falsifier:** two settlements of one seed, of different acceptances or demands, sharing `rho_out`; a wallet that, after a
read showing a held note spent by a statement it never saved, proves or signs a new statement before `recordRestoration`;
or a lit wallet that pays a request whose exact output a statement spending its own notes already created.

## 2026-10-07 — Restore a wallet directory from a copy by a recorded restoration, and audit copied views (slice 13 M13e)

**Status:** accepted after design and integrated review.
**Spec change:** none needed. lit-v1 §8 already makes a wallet that cannot see which indices it exposed read every
index through `h + 256` as exposed, and C4.5 leaves accounting acknowledgements to the wallet's own backup.

**Question:** slice 13 (c) asks for backup and restore drills of every role's store, and `moe venue audit` for restored
views; the durable-operation gate asks that "restored backups and obsolete instances cannot cause conflicting exposed
signatures or silently lose accepted operations". M13d covered the operator. A probe copied each other role's
directory at T0, let the original keep working, then used the copy with ordinary commands:
- (F1, high, lit wallet) the copy gave a new request the owner key its lost instance gave another, against lit-v1 §8
  ("each request names its own owner key"): one payer's payment credited another's order;
- (F2, high, both constructions) the copy fulfilled again a request its lost instance had credited;
- (F3, high, lit) an exact `pay` retry after a restoration (or `restore-seed`) paid a request twice: pool-v3 refuses a
  request whose exact output exists, but a lit request names only a key, and no read indexes outputs by payee key;
- (F4, medium) a copied `replay.db` carries a self-consistent digest, so its kept classes are reused without replay
  (pool-v3 §14 wants the digest in storage only the reader writes);
- (F5, medium) a copied `venue.db` is trusted: reopening checks only what is cheap, and nothing ran `ErgoVenue.audit`;
- (F6, low–medium, pool-v3) a copy can settle a demand at the disclosure count of a release its lost instance has
  unwitnessed in a gap (C3.5), which a backer holding that release can use; seed restoration shares it;
- (F7, low) a relay's `spend.db` rolls back, and its outbox forgets pending transactions.
Already safe: pool-v3 requests (random identifiers) and payment retries, K's issues and acceptances (derived
deterministically), demands and withdrawals, `evidence.db` (authenticated on use), a replica (signs nothing).

**Decision:**
1. *A wallet restoration is the owner's step.* `moe wallet restore --copy --dir <d>` (`V3Wallet.recordRestoration`) runs
   before anything else on a restored directory, in one transaction:
   - every request not fulfilled then is marked (`receiver_restored`, exported in a handoff and checked at install);
     `fulfill` refuses it `RESTORED` unless the holder's records outside the wallet show it was not credited
     (`--uncredited`), and fulfilling clears the mark [F2];
   - a lit wallet reads every owner key's exposure as unknown, so its next read of each backing, a backing first read
     after the restoration included, exposes every index through `h + 256` and `move-window` comes before new
     requests: the existing seed-restoration rule [F1];
   - the file becomes the wallet's own.
   Recording again is harmless (it marks the requests unfulfilled then; a read at an unchanged `h` exposes the same
   indices).
2. *File identity is a guard, not the mechanism* (M13d item 6, moved to `src/file-identity.ts`). The wallet keeps its
   file's identity (`wallet_file`, never exported) and refuses every acting operation and a new handoff `COPIED` until
   item 1; reads stay open. The design review showed that on Linux a directory removed and copied back can take the
   freed inodes again, besides in-place overwrites and snapshot rollbacks, so the procedure says to run item 1 after any
   restore and the gate does not rest on detection.
3. *Caches restored from a copy are read again.* A kept replay file's digest file names the file's identity beside the
   page digest, so a copy's kept state is discarded and replayed from nothing [F4]. A durable Ergo view keeps its
   file's identity and audits every row with work before any read where it is another file's, refusing with the remedy
   (remove the file, sync from the anchor) where a row does not reproduce [F5].
4. *`moe venue audit --dir <d>`* re-checks any role's view (`ErgoVenue.audit` with work), reading no node and changing
   nothing; `AUDIT` names the first row that does not reproduce. It covers what identity misses.
5. *A relay keeps no state another party relies on* [F7]: holders and backers resend exact files, and the funding key's
   balance is the hard cap on what a restored `spend.db` cannot count. A restore step with a stated budget was proposed
   and dropped in review as a mechanism that guards only fees.
6. *Open, recorded as the gate's remaining wallet items:* an obsolete instance coming back beside the restored copy (or a
   seed restoration beside a live wallet) is not fenced; F3, also in flight; F6. Each needs more than this slice:
   - a fence from public evidence (a spend of a held note no saved record reserves, or a lit output to an own key above
     `exposed`), which a seed restoration must not trip on its own history;
   - for F3, a payee-key index over the canonical trails, or a wait past the restoration index plus twice the lag for
     what is in flight;
   - for F6, local policy (no gap release for a demand older than the restoration) or a later version binding `rho_out`
     to the acceptance (the review found a wait does not help, since the backer may hold the lost release).
7. Wallet profiles move to `moe/wallet/v3/9` and `moe/wallet/keyed/2`. No retained state holds a moe wallet.

**Rationale and alternatives:**
- *Reuse seed restoration wholesale:* loses every saved payment, receipt and alias the copy does hold.
- *One directory-level guard:* the same inode reuse, and library users have no directory.
- *Refuse a copied wallet for good:* the copy is the owner's money; a handoff needs the source alive.
- Costs: one stat per open of each store, two small tables, one full replay of a copied replay file, one audit of a
  copied view (linear in its rows, work included), and a lit window move after a restoration.

**Evidence:**
- `test/lit-wallet.test.ts`: a copy refuses `COPIED` for a request, a fulfillment and a new handoff; its restoration
  marks the open invoice, refuses a request until a read, then `WINDOW` past `h + 256` (the lost instance's key is
  index 1); the credited invoice refuses `RESTORED`, fulfills with `uncredited` and clears its mark; recording again
  exposes nothing new; a handoff of a restored wallet carries its marks.
- `test/pool-v3-wallet-backup.test.ts`: the same on a pool-v3 wallet (a copy refuses `COPIED`; the credited request
  refuses `RESTORED` and fulfills with `uncredited`). `test/lit-wallet.test.ts` also hands off a restored wallet
  holding a saved window move before its first read.
- `test/pool-v3-kept-state.test.ts`: a copied kept file is discarded and read in full. `test/ergo-store.test.ts`: a
  faithful copied view opens audited and is its own file after; a changed copy refuses with the remedy; the same change
  in place passes reopening and `audit` finds it; a copied failed view keeps its failure, and a copied unsettled view
  refuses a damaged header row by name.
- The lit command drill (separate installs): the shop's wallet, a reader and a relay are lost after T0 and their
  backups restored in place; the wallet refuses `COPIED`, `restore --copy` lists the credited invoice, `fulfill`
  refuses `RESTORED`, the read exposes the window and `move-window` precedes a request whose key differs from the lost
  instance's; the reader reads the same supply; `venue audit` passes, then refuses a view changed in place (`AUDIT`),
  whose removal resyncs; the relay answers its earlier publication final. The pool-v3 command drill's operator restore
  is unchanged.
- Not covered: pool-v3 wallet restoration with real proofs (the gate is the same code path), Windows and macOS file
  identity, and the open items of item 6.

**Review:** a probe (one reviewer) found F1–F7. A fresh reviewer then reviewed the proposal:
- *High:* identity misses an ordinary Linux restore (rm and copy back reuse inodes). Taken: item 2.
- *High:* no fence against a second live instance. Recorded as open (item 6), with the proposed fence.
- *Medium:* F3's check at read time races a payment in flight, and matching only the key lets anyone block a payer.
  Taken: F3 left open with both fixes named.
- *Medium:* F6's proposed wait does not close it. Taken: item 6.
- *Low–medium:* the relay budget restore is unnecessary. Taken: item 5.
- *Low:* identity cannot meet §14 against a directory another party supplies; `venue audit` checks the directory's own
  venue file. Documented in the venue guide (the kept terms name the venue).
- *Low:* a retried restoration and a handoff from a copy. Taken: item 1, and a copy refuses a new handoff.
A fresh reviewer then reviewed the integrated patch (e4f42d3..e245104) and found every acting path fenced, the
restoration atomic, the replay digest stable under SQLite's in-place writes and the relaxed install check opening
nothing a handoff could not already carry. Findings, all taken:
- *Medium–high:* a restored lit wallet holding a saved window move, handed off before its first read, froze and its
  handoff never restored (the move record's install check still required a known exposure). Both checks now read an
  unknown exposure as `h + 256`; a test hands such a wallet off.
- *Low–medium:* a copied view in venue failure was told to remove itself, which would drop the evidence that finality
  broke. Its header rows are audited and the failure kept; `venue audit` refuses it with the failure (`VENUE`).
- *Low:* a copied view that never settled refused a damaged header row with an unnamed error; now a named refusal.
- *Low:* no pool-v3 wallet test of `COPIED`, `RESTORED` and `uncredited`; added.
- *Cosmetic:* an orphaned comment; and a restored lit wallet's payment or burn with change also waits for the window
  move, now said in the note and the guide.
- *Suspected:* a first read after the restoration from a view older than the lost instance's fixes the exposure lower
  than that instance's (shared with seed restoration under lit-v1 §8). The procedure asks for a view at least as fresh.

**Falsifier:** a restored wallet directory that, after `restore --copy`, names an owner key its lost instance exposed or
fulfills a request without `--uncredited` that existed unfulfilled at the restoration; or a copied replay file or view
whose rows are reused without a replay or an audit where its file identity changed.

## 2026-10-07 — Restore an operator journal by a return at a skipped sequence once silence is witnessed (slice 13 M13d)

**Status:** accepted after review and read-back.
**Spec change:** [Construction C2.4.1 and C2.7.5 at 25c078e](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/commit/25c078e3df85ea39621b86ab6997449afb82929e).
No pool-v3 byte, identity or verdict changes: §13.3 already reads a sequence between held sequences as one the record
moved past.

**Question:** slice 13 (c) requires that an operator restored from an older copy signs nothing conflicting. The
durable-operation gate ([production requirements](../docs/PRODUCTION_REQUIREMENTS.md#release-gates)) requires that restored
backups and obsolete instances cannot cause conflicting exposed signatures. C2.8.1 resumes a restarted journal from the
book it held, assuming that book is durable. A copy breaks that premise, and nothing enforced the comment "an operator
directory is never restored from a copy". Probes on main at 366b4e9 copied a closed journal and reopened the original:
- (A) The original co-signed a demand after its last commitment and was lost. The copy co-signed a different demand at
  the same position of the same segment: two conflicting exposed receipts.
- (B) The original committed again and the venue witnessed it. The copy refused every command `CONFLICT`.

**Decision:**
1. *Return only, at a skipped sequence.* `restored: true` (`moe operator restore`) records a fence: the venue index, the
   log's tip and a spacing r drawn uniformly from [0, 2³¹). From then on the journal signs nothing but C2b.4's return,
   once the scope's silence boundary is witnessed, at S, plus that return's adopted block. Adopting the return at S
   lifts the fence.
   - S is the higher of the journal's own last signed sequence and the record's latest held sequence for this key, plus
     2¹⁶·(1 + r).
   - What was co-signed after the last witnessed commitment lapses at the return (C2b.4: "never final").
   - A return pending when the copy was made is adopted as the lost instance would adopt it (the same record gives the
     same receipts), and the fence stands.
2. *Deny by default.* `submit` of a new statement, `commit`, `open`, `rescope` and `takeover` refuse `RESTORED`, as do
   a second return and an adopt of any opening but those above. Serving, `audit`, `status` and `publish` stay open,
   and exact repeats keep their stored replies.
3. *A restoration recorded again* before its return is signed changes nothing (corrected by review-code 2026-10-08: it
   keeps its index and draws a fresh spacing, since a copy taken while the fence stands holds the same row). Once the return is signed, every
   recording is a restoration of its own: the journal drops the signed fence and draws a fresh one. The pending return
   is then published and adopted, keeping the fence, and the next return skips past it. A backup taken after the first
   restoration's return was signed may stand beside an instance that adopted that return and co-signed in its segment,
   and a file's identity cannot tell such a copy rolled back in place from a retry. So after `pending` the next step is
   `adopt`, never `restore`. Under a fence, a return identifier answers only that fence's own return.
4. *An obsolete instance publishes nothing under the restoration.* `publish()`, and so `serve`, refuses `CONFLICT` when
   the record holds a later commitment of this key that it did not sign. Its own bytes at a held sequence are only
   republished. Signing paths already refuse such a record (`exclusive`).
5. *The skip is recorded* (`journal_skipped`).
   - Serving's signed-row walk passes exactly that gap, including for a reader served through a sequence inside it.
   - Reloading's row count passes it too, and each skip must span from a signed row to the return with none inside.
     Without the count, every restart after a restoration refused `STORAGE`; a test caught it.
   - A commitment of the key at most 16 past the skip's base is the lost instance's landing late, not a conflict: the
     record moves past it. Higher in the gap it is another signer's (invariant 22). Landing after the return is
     signed but before it is held, it holds in the return's place with lost evidence: the backing reads unresolved and
     recovery is succession, a timing anyone holding that transaction can choose (the read-back's limit).
6. *Copy detection is a guard, not the mechanism.* The journal keeps its file's inode, and its birth time on systems
   that report a real one (not Linux, where libuv without statx reports the change time; not the device, which a remount
   can change), and refuses `COPIED` on a mismatch; the remedy is `restore`.
   - It catches a fresh copy or a restore to another disk or machine.
   - It misses an in-place overwrite and a filesystem or VM snapshot rollback, which the
     [operator guide](../docs/POOL_V3_SERVICE.md#restoring-an-operator-directory) makes the owner's procedure.
   - It does not count toward the gate.
7. *Case (B) is unchanged:* `CONFLICT`. Recovery is succession under a successor term, or later the lever of importing
   the newer evidence. A backing whose terms declare no silence clause never has a gap to return from, so it too needs
   succession. Where neither is possible, the outage is permanent.
8. The journal profile moves to `pool-store/v3/8` (three tables). No retained state holds an operator journal.

**Review:** one reviewer, two rounds, reproducing on 757f98b.

The first proposal (return after silence at the next sequence, a list of refused commands, detection by device, inode and
birth time) was rejected:
- *Critical:* the copy's return took the next sequence, which the original may already have signed, published and
  served unwitnessed. That is an equivocation (C2.4.1), and the original's later receipts then read `invalid-receipt`.
  Taken: the skip.
- *High:* `publish()` never checked the record, and an obsolete original holding a byte-identical pending return
  resumed beside the copy. Taken: item 4 and the skip.
- *High:* `rescope` and `takeover` were not refused; a copy's `rescope` abandons the original's tail (C2.10.9b). Taken:
  deny by default.
- *Medium:* detection misses in-place restores and can misfire. Taken: item 6.
- *Medium:* the cost was understated. A backing with no silence clause never returns, and C2.10.9a's repair, which
  needs no silence, was not compared. The repair was then proposed.

The read-back accepted the skip and its random spacing.
- *Bound:* the one-in-flight rules leave at most an ordinary commitment and a pending opening unwitnessed.
- *Randomness is needed:* without it two restorations of one backup sign identical openings.
- *Normative:* it found that the repair conflicts with pool-authority C2.10.9 ("a new segment may not be used to turn a
  live receipt into an excused one"). C2.4.3's premise holds only if the lost instance actually signed a later
  commitment, and pool-v3 instantiates that contract, which may change only for a later version. Taken: v3 restores
  by return alone. The repair is a later version's lever. That also removes a race the read-back confirmed: the
  original's in-flight commitment mined between the repair's signing and its witnessing.
- *High:* a backup taken after the restoration's return was signed reused S. Taken: item 3.
- Open there and now covered by tests: the reload across the skip, and a return pending when the copy was made.
- Not covered: lit-v1 journals, multi-backing scopes, and Windows file identity.

A fresh reviewer then reviewed the integrated patch (366b4e9..00e4ecf) and asked for fixes before merge.
- *High:* the CLI treated a matching file identity with a signed return as a retry. After an in-place rollback, which
  the guide covers, that let the copy adopt the lost instance's return and co-sign in its segment beside it. Taken:
  item 3.
- *Medium:* a reader marked inside the skip was refused `STORAGE`. Taken: item 5.
- *Medium:* Linux without statx reports the change time as birth time, which would refuse every restart `COPIED`.
  Taken: item 6.
- *Medium:* the lost instance's in-flight commitment landing late stopped the journal for good. Taken: item 5, inside
  the skip; before the return is signed it remains case (B).
- *Low–medium:*
  - `restore` never published a pending opening. Taken.
  - An earlier restoration's return identifier answered the old return while the fence silently stood. Taken: item 3.
- *Low:*
  - A damaged skip row could offset a lost row. Taken.
  - The CLI leaked the operator if `status` threw, and matched message text. Taken (a `SILENCE` check).
  - An unused column. Dropped.
  - The drill accepted an early return. Taken: it now requires `restored` first.
  - "resubmit" should be "reprove". Taken.

The read-back of those fixes found them sound and asked for two more, both taken: exempt only a band of 16 past the
skip's base (a stolen key signing elsewhere in the gap stays a conflict), and pass over the lost commitment in serving
rather than report it as damage, with the case where it lands before the return is held stated as a limit.

**Rationale and alternatives:**
- *Succession only:* needs a successor term the backer signs and a new key for every restore.
- *Commit before co-signing:* a commitment omitting a receipted statement contradicts its receipt (C2.8.1).
- *Resume after the lag when the record shows nothing newer:* this is case (A).
- *A liveness record on the venue:* a new kind with a cost at every start, still blind to an unpublished tail.
- *Repair before silence:* see the review.
- Costs:
  - A restoration costs the silence duration and earns C2b.6's grade meanwhile.
  - S consumes at most about 2⁴⁷ of the 2⁶⁴ sequence space per restoration.
  - One stat per open and three small tables.

**Evidence:** `test/pool-v3-recovery-store.test.ts`:
- (A): refused `COPIED`; every other command refused `RESTORED`; a return before silence refused `STALE`; the return at S
  is no equivocation with the lost unwitnessed commitment; the lost tail's receipts, including one given after that
  commitment, read `lapsed`; `audit`, serving and reloading pass across the skip; the obsolete instance is refused
  `CONFLICT` at `publish` and `submit`.
- (B): `CONFLICT` at `return` and `publish`.
- A return pending in the copy is adopted with the fence kept, then the copy's own return lifts it.
- A copy taken after the restoration's return was signed draws a fresh fence and returns past it, refusing the earlier
  return's identifier.
- A reader served through a sequence inside the skip is served; the lost commitment landing late is no conflict and its
  tail still reads `lapsed`; a damaged skip row is refused `STORAGE`.
- The pool-v3 command drill runs it through separate installs: a copy restored in place of the operator directory
  refuses `COPIED`; `restore` answers `restored` before silence, then returns at a skipped sequence; `open` is
  refused `RESTORED`; `adopt`; serve commits past the return.

**Falsifier:** a sequence of copies, restorations and restarts under which any instance signs two roots at one sequence
or two receipts at one position of one segment, or a genuine receipt of the lost tail reads invalid rather than lapsed.

## 2026-10-07 — Repair a read a source left unresolved: from nothing, then from the next source (slice 13 M13b, audit 30 (au))

**Status:** accepted after review; no specification change. Closes (au).

**Question:** a source's mark moves once its answer holds the selection's directory, snapshots and trails
([audit 30](#2026-10-07--move-a-sources-mark-only-past-an-answer-that-delivers-its-selection-and-charge-a-streams-rate-to-the-peer-alone-audit-area-30)).
A source that sends the selection whole but withholds an earlier dependency (an opening checkpoint's directory, what an
opening took, a terms field) still moves it. Later syncs from that source ask only after the mark, so a holder's read
stays unresolved. Only a `full` sync repairs it, and no command passes `full`. Because the source answered, the
replicas are never tried. `V3Wallet.supply` documents the remedy ("where a read stays unresolved over what the file
holds, supply it again in full"), but the commands never take it.

**Decision:** one helper, `readRepaired` in `cli/reader.ts`, runs every command read over served evidence: the wallet's
reads (`withEvidence`), `reader supply` and `presentation`, and a replica's rounds. When the read ends
`unresolved-evidence` over a source's answer, that source is synced again from nothing and the read runs again. When
that read is still unresolved, the source is passed over as `UNRESOLVED` (named in the output's `skipped`) and the next
source is asked, until a read resolves. Each source is asked from nothing at most once per selection it serves. The
directory's `unresolved.json` records, per backing and by each source's normalized URL, the highest selection at which
that source's answer from nothing stayed unresolved. While the source serves that selection or a lower one, it is asked
only after its mark and passed over at once if its read is still unresolved. A resolved read clears the source's entry,
and the record is written best effort. When no source is left, the read refuses `unresolved-evidence`. The refusal
names the sources passed over and the remedy: removing `unresolved.json` asks every source from nothing again. That
remedy is for a recorded selection that will never move (a quiet or closed backing) and for a holder file damaged
since the record was made. Any other failure ends the read as before. A read run again
is the library's exact retry: each command's mutation comes after its read.

**Rationale:**
- *Move a mark only after a resolved read* (restore the mark from before the sync when the read is unresolved): an
  unresolved read for another reason (a venue with no answer reads the same) would then fetch the same growing range on
  every command, and the replicas would still go untried while the operator answers.
- *A complete dependency check in `sync`:* it would be a second reader of what the read already judges.
- The cost falls on unresolved reads alone: one answer from nothing per source and selection, each bounded by the
  store's quota per response. A read that stays unresolved (every source withholding, or the holder's own venue view or
  storage failing, which reads the same status) pays it again only when a source's selection moves, not on every command
  or replica round. While the holder's own view or storage is at fault, honest sources are named `UNRESOLVED`.
- *An in-memory record:* each wallet command is its own process, so the record must be kept in the directory to bound
  the cost across commands.

**Evidence:** `test/replica.test.ts`, on a lit operator, its replica and a wallet:
- A source that withholds the segment's terms on every answer leaves the plain read unresolved. The repaired read asks
  it again from nothing, passes it over as `UNRESOLVED` and resolves from the replica.
- A source that lost the field once is repaired by its own answer from nothing.
- With no source left the refusal names both sources, and the record holds each one's selection. The next read asks
  each only after its mark. Once the operator's selection moves, it is asked from nothing once more and its entry
  clears. Another refusal (`unsupported-scope`) is not retried.
- `check:lit:commands`: a holder's evidence file with the segment's terms removed past its mark makes `wallet sync`
  resolve from the operator from nothing. With the `full` wiring removed from the build, the step fails with
  `UNRESOLVED`, naming the source.
- Patch review (a fresh reviewer, one reproducer):
  - Major, taken: an unresolved read re-fetched everything from every source on every command or round. Now it does
    so once per selection.
  - Minors, taken: with replicas and no service file, running out of sources refused as `ABSENT` (`syncSources` now
    judges the service file against every added replica); only a hand-written `supply` exercised the wiring (now
    covered by the drill step); the final refusal did not name the sources tried.
  - Nits, not taken: the kept `packages/<backing>` may hold an unresolved package, as before; `skipped` lists the
    `UNRESOLVED` entries first, and an unanswering operator is asked once per round.
  - Suspicion recorded, not built: a hostile replica asked first might leave fork rows that make an honest later
    source read unresolved (related to (aq)).
  - Read-back of the fixes: no blockers. Minors taken: a replica URL written with a default port missed its record
    (sources are now keyed by the client's normalized URL); a source alternating its selection was pulled in full
    each time (only a rising selection is recorded now); a recorded source whose selection never moves strands the
    read (the refusal and the guide now name the remedy); a failed record write could replace a result or refusal
    (now best effort). Sound: the bound per source and rising selection across commands and rounds, backings kept
    apart, the record's parsing, `writeReplace`'s atomic rename under the directory's process lock.
  - Found sound: termination (at most two supply calls per source); exact retries (every wallet mutation comes after
    its read); `ReplayStore` reuse after a refused read; `replica.keep` and printing only after a resolved read.

Unit suite 1,347 passed; `check:lit:commands` passed.

## 2026-10-07 — Pin a release's install to the tested tree with an install lock, and rebuild it byte-identically on two systems (slice 13 M13a)

**Status:** accepted (tooling and packaging; no specification or runtime change).

**Question:** the release assurance gate asks for reproducible builds and pinned dependencies. What must a release carry
so that anyone can check that the code they install is exactly what its commit builds and tests, and what is the
smallest mechanism? Probes on 2026-10-07 (Node 24.21.0, npm 11.19.0):
- Two clean Linux builds of one commit from different directories gave one tarball (SHA-1 061e667). Nothing in
  `tsc`'s output or `npm pack`'s archive depends on the path or the time.
- A consumer's `npm install` of that tarball resolved `pako` 3.0.2 under `noir_js`, while the checkout's lockfile and
  every test ran 3.0.1. The pool-v3 command drill installed the same way, so the commands it accepts were not the tree
  the unit suite ran.
- With the lockfile shipped as `npm-shrinkwrap.json`, the tarball install still resolved 3.0.2, and a consumer's own
  `@noble/curves` 1.9.0 satisfied the package's `^1.8.0`.

**Decision:**
1. A release is the tarball plus an install lock (`scripts/release.mjs`): a package.json and package-lock.json naming
   the tarball by its SHA-512 integrity and each runtime entry of the checkout's lockfile, unchanged at its path.
   `npm ci --ignore-scripts` in that directory installs the tested tree or refuses, save that it drops an optional entry
   failing its integrity, which the check below refuses.
2. `check:package` builds it in `scratch/release/`, installs it and checks npm's record of what it placed
   (`verifyInstall`): every installed entry is the pinned one, and every pinned entry for this system's `os` and `cpu`
   is there, optional ones included. It runs the installed package's checks and writes `release-record.json`: the
   commit built and whether its tree was clean, the tarball's bytes, SHA-256 and integrity, the install lock's and
   checkout lockfile's SHA-256, the specification pins the shipped constructions name, and Node's and npm's versions.
   An installer runs the same check as `node scripts/release.mjs --verify <directory>` from a checkout of the commit.
3. CI uploads each system's record from `check`, and a `reproducible-release` job requires the Linux and Windows
   records to agree on everything but the toolchain line.
4. The pool-v3 command drill installs the commands it runs through the same function.
5. The package's direct `dependencies` become exact versions (the two `@noble` ranges joined the two exact pins). The
   `vite` development pin, kept for the lockfile the reports bind (2026-09 decision), goes with this lockfile change;
   vitest still resolves `vite` 7.3.6.

**Rationale:**
- *A shrinkwrap in the tarball:* it did not pin a tarball install, and only a registry publication (outside this
  authority) could show that it pins a registry install.
- *Bundled dependencies:* the tarball would carry about 170 MB, `@aztec/bb.js` alone 152 MB, and the native
  `msgpackr-extract` build of one system. The install lock pins the same bytes by integrity without shipping them.
- *Exact pins alone:* they fix four direct dependencies and leave about twenty transitive ones floating.
- *A committed expected hash:* it would change with every source change. Comparing two systems' builds of one commit
  tests reproduction without one.
- Cost: one module of about 120 lines, one CI job of seconds, and `npm ci` instead of `npm install` in two checks.

**Evidence:**
- `npm run check:package` passes and records 23 runtime entries. The installed tree matches the lock entry for entry,
  with `pako` 3.0.1.
- A tarball repacked with one line appended to `dist/index.js` fails `npm ci` from the install lock with `EINTEGRITY`
  given an empty cache. Given a cache holding the genuine tarball, npm installs the genuine bytes by integrity.
- Patch review (a fresh reviewer, reproducers on a copy of the package): one major, taken. npm ci drops an optional
  entry whose bytes fail their integrity and exits 0; the native `msgpackr-extract` build was then missing and the old
  check, which skipped optional entries, passed. `verifyInstall` now requires the optional entries for this system and
  installers have `--verify` (reproduced: refused; the genuine install passes). Minors taken: the record names the
  commit and whether its tree was clean; the package may declare no optional, peer or bundled dependencies (its lock
  entry carries `dependencies` alone); the command drill's report field is now `installLockSha256`. Nits taken: the
  comparison prints both toolchains first. Found sound: both systems' records in CI run 37650769335 byte-identical and
  matching a local Linux build; LF output, `portable` packing, fixed mtimes and normalized modes; npm ci's refusals of
  a missing nested entry (`EUSAGE`) and of a wrong required integrity; the `dev` filter for this lock (no
  `devOptional`, peer, bundled or linked entries, which the assertion refuses); placement of each entry where the tests
  ran it. `main` has no branch protection, so `reproducible-release` binds through the practice of merging only a
  green run, not a required check.
- `npx moe` in the installed directory prints the usage and exits 2.
- The Linux and Windows comparison is CI's (this PR's run).
- Limits: reproduction is checked for one Node and npm release at a time; registry availability is assumed; install
  scripts are not run; no registry publication is made or tested; npm configurations that omit optional entries or
  name another `os`/`cpu`, macOS and ARM are untested.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-07 — Move a source's mark only past an answer that delivers its selection, and charge a stream's rate to the peer alone (audit, area 30)

**Status:** accepted. This is the rolling audit of the v3 service transport: `service-wire.ts`, `service-http.ts`,
`service-client.ts`, `replica.ts`, the replica's serving in `evidence-store.ts` and `cli/relay.ts`'s listener and send.
No specification change.

**Question:** is the transport sound against a hostile peer on either side, as pool-v3 §§10, 12.1 and 14 (incremental
retrieval: "a supplier's stated position or length authenticates nothing") and the engineering contract (named
refusals, no endless wait) require? Two independent reviewers found four defects.
- A source's mark moved past evidence the store never received. `take` returned true for any parts that broke no
  trail continuation, an empty answer included. A source stating the operator's published sequence and sending
  nothing moved the operator's mark, which is one for every URL and backing. Later honest syncs served only what
  came after it. A quiet or closed backing stayed unresolved for good, and no command asks from nothing.
- The server measured a stream's minimum rate (64 bytes/ms after 15 s) from its start. A source slower than that
  rate (slow rows, a loaded replica) cut off a fast honest peer at the same point on every retry.
- The client refreshed its 10 s stall bound on every chunk and had no rate bound. A source dripping one byte at a
  time held a sync forever, with no refusal.
- `wholePackage` spread a trail's chunks as arguments, so a source sending about 130,000 chunks raised a RangeError.

**Decision:**
1. `sync` asks from nothing where, after a source's answer, the store does not hold the selection's directory, each
   snapshot it names and each snapshot's trail (`holdsSelection`: one row and one chain step each). Where an
   answer from nothing still lacks them, the source is refused as not assembling, so a holder's command passes to the
   next source and the mark stays. This is the condition an honest journal or replica meets, since each serves every
   directory signed in range with its snapshots and trails. It covers the selection only. A source that sends the
   selection whole but leaves out an earlier dependency (an opening checkpoint's directory, what an opening took, a
   terms field) still moves the mark, and only `full` repairs it: deferred as (au).
2. One rule, `servedTooSlow`, bounds a stream on both sides: the time one side spent waiting on the other passes 15 s
   plus 1 ms per 64 bytes moved. The server counts only its writes' waits on backpressure. The client counts only its
   waits for chunks, and stops as a source that did not answer (`TimeoutError`, read as `UNAVAILABLE`). The client
   cannot tell a slow source from a slow path to it, so the reference client passes over a source slower than the rule
   past fifteen seconds; the server's change serves any peer that reads at the rate.
3. `wholePackage` copies a trail's chunks in one pass.
4. The listeners check their one bearer credential in one helper.

**Rationale:**
- Mark. Keying the operator's mark by URL would not help, because the operator itself, or its service on a damaged
  store, can send the empty answer. Requiring at least one part would refuse an honest keep-alive commitment whose
  directory is unchanged. A CLI retry with `full` after an unresolved read would also work, but it adds a second
  exchange to every unresolved read and a flag to every command. The check reads only what the store already holds,
  so the mark never claims the selection while the store lacks it.
- Rate. A deadline for the whole stream would cut off long honest histories. Counting each side's waits on the other
  makes the bound depend on the peer alone. The server's 15 s timer still ends a stalled peer and a source silent
  for 15 s.

**Evidence:**
- Tests fail on main and pass with the fixes: a source dripping a terabyte trail (client, `TimeoutError`); a source
  taking 2 s a chunk served whole to a fast peer (server); a peer that keeps reading below the rate cut off (server;
  it fails with the check removed); a trail in 200,000 chunks (`wholePackage`). On the mark:
  a withheld directory moves no mark, from the mark or from nothing; an empty answer over a held selection moves it.
  The journal test (`pool-v3-store.test.ts`) and the wallet test (`pool-v3-wallet-kept.test.ts`) that recorded the old
  gap (a store whose rows were lost read unresolved until `full`) now repair on an ordinary sync.
- Unit suite 1,346 passed; typecheck; `check:scripts`; PR CI green on its real-proof groups (run 37642694106).
- Patch review (a fresh reviewer): one major taken as a limit, (au) below. A source that withholds an earlier
  dependency while sending the selection whole still moves the mark (reproduced). Two minors taken: the client's
  rate claim is narrowed, and the server's cut-off of a slow peer now has a test.
- Reviewers found sound: the served frame's arithmetic and bounds; authorization (duplicate headers, timing, a
  holders' listener with no admin path); route matching; the loopback check; JSON intake and canonical re-encoding;
  receipt and commitment binding; `proxyExempts` against Node 24's bundled undici; the relay's queue and budget;
  selection pinning; truncation; the replica's `from` computation, fork handling, index races and migration; and the
  part bounds.

**Deferred** (WORK.md Next 4):
- (ap) A terms field a replica learns later changes no indexed object or top, so it is never served past a mark. A
  reader behind a chained replica then stays unresolved (index heads, serve a count-zero trail).
- (aq) A hostile upstream's kind-4 junk is indexed and served on by a replica. It extends the recorded storage limit
  (2026-10-01, M4) to every reader of that replica, and can push one past its byte budget.
- (ar) A backing whose canonical sequence lags another's of the same operator is never kept by a replica (record
  per-backing selections, and serve such a backing from nothing).
- (as) Two implementations serve §14's parts: the journal's `parts()` and the replica's `served()`. They share the
  paging, packing and base logic, and differ on (o). Supplying the journal's rows to one `serveParts` would close (o).
- (at) `relayRefusal` maps `VenueError` by message text (a code on the publisher's two lasting refusals).
- (au) A source behind the operator's credential that sends the selection whole but withholds an earlier dependency
  still moves the operator's mark. Later reads stay unresolved until a `full` sync, and no command passes `full`.
  Because the source answered, the replicas are never tried. The fix is one of two: move a mark only after a read over
  the synced store resolves (the CLI restores the mark from before the sync where that read is unresolved), or sync
  once more with `full` and then try the replicas. Either touches every wallet command's read.
- The client's strict `content-type` refuses `; charset=utf-8` (interoperability only).

## 2026-10-07 — Fund a holder's gap act through a third party's relay reached as an onion service, under one credential per relay (slice 12 M12c)

**Status:** accepted after review; delivery is in WORK.md.

**Question:** visibility duty 2, the last of slice 12's acceptance (c): keep the funding of a holder's gap publications
(a demand, its withdrawal or release) apart from any identified coins. On Ergo each publication is an output of a
transaction spending its publisher's boxes (venue-ergo §6), so the chain ties the record to the funding key, to that
key's other publications and to where its coins came from, "the largest metadata leak in the profile" for a holder's
gap acts ([visibility](../docs/POOL_V3_VISIBILITY.md)); issuer and witness together identify a presenter funded from
an identified address, though its presenter keys are fresh per demand. `moe relay publish` published from the relay
directory's one key: a relay of the holder's own linked its gap acts and its coins, and a third party's relay was
reachable only by carrying the file to its operator by hand.

**Decision:**
1. *A third party's relay funds the act; the holder's own relay per demand is the fallback.* No new cryptography and
   no byte, identity or verdict change: anyone may publish a gap act unchanged (C3.3a; pool-recovery: "anyone may relay
   the unchanged demand").
2. *`moe relay serve [--port <p>] [--onion <host>] [--poll-ms <ms>]`* runs the relay directory's publisher and view in
   one process. A loop syncs the view each poll, settling pending publications; it listens only once a first sync has
   passed. Its one route, `POST /publications`, takes the `moe-publication-1` file `moe wallet publish` or
   `publish-acceptance` writes and judges it as `relay publish` does (`VENUE`, `INVALID`, `CONFIGURATION`, `SUBJECT`;
   `final` once witnessed; `EARLY` for a demand whose instant the view has not reached; `BUDGET`), then answers `pending`
   with the transaction. The loop and requests take one turn at a time; two requests wait at most beside the work
   running (others `BUSY`), and one whose connection is gone by its turn is dropped unjudged. A resend reuses the
   publisher's pending transaction (its key omits the height), so it answers the same until the publisher rebuilds it.
   The publisher's refusals no resend passes are `TOO_LARGE` and `FULL`. A publisher whose persistence failed ends
   `serve` with `STORAGE`.
