- Package copying before the first await.

**Spec change:** none needed.
## 2026-10-06 — Take lit's acts and §8's window move in the one wallet, presented notes spending as any other (slice 14 M14g2)

**Status:** accepted after review; delivery is in WORK.md.

**Question:** M14g1 ([decision](#2026-10-06--hold-lit-notes-in-the-one-wallet-with-owner-keys-per-backing-found-under-a-doubling-window-slice-14-m14g),
item 7) left lit's acts and §8's window move to M14g2: issue with K's kept nonce, demand, accept, settle refusing an
acceptance owner that is one of the wallet's keys, withdraw, burn, freshen and publish, and the window move by a
self-payment; until then a lit wallet restored from its seed could not request (its window full) and paid only without
change. How does the one wallet take them without a second implementation of custody, saving, resolution, submission
or publication?

**Decision:**
1. *Acts are saved records as the pool's.* Each lit act is one saved record under the one alias namespace, with its
   reservations, statuses, receipt and publication, saved by the one `saveAct` (which may build the record inside its
   transaction, so an index a burn's change allocates is saved with it or not at all). The construction supplies how a
   record is built and signed (`KeyedWalletFrames`: burn, demand, withdraw, settle, issue, acceptance, publication). A
   payment's fate and an act's are read through the construction's `StatementView` (segment, outputs, a demand's
   instant, a settlement's acceptance deadline) in place of pool-v3's public-input indices, so one resolution rule
   serves both constructions; pool-v3's view fields are exactly the inputs the rule read, so its verdicts are unchanged.
   A lit settlement's view names its output only while its demand stands, and the rule fails a settlement whose demand
   no longer stands before reading it.
2. *Issue (§§2, 8).* K's wallet derives the nonce from its seed: `HMAC-SHA256(issueRoot, backing || owner || u64
   quantity)`, `issueRoot = HKDF-SHA256(seed, salt=domain, info="moe/wallet/lit/v1/issue")`; §8 requires a deterministic
   derivation and leaves it to K. One issuance per backing, request key and quantity: an issue whose segment ended fails
   and is made again under a new alias with the same output, a copy restored from the seed builds the same record, and
   an issue whose output exists is refused (CONFLICT) before K signs. An issue saved here and not failed that names the
   key refuses another with any quantity (a request key is paid once); one to the wallet's own key is refused
   (`OWN_KEY`: it would credit no request); the key check runs again where the issue saves, so two calls racing cannot both save. §8's "credited unless the wallet itself made it": an output no statement
   consumed notes for whose `rho` is the issue derivation over this seed's nonce is the wallet's own issue, credited to
   no request; the test needs only the seed.
3. *Burn.* The smallest available note or pair covering the quantity, signed by its owners; the rest to the backing's
   next index (WINDOW where the window is full), no change output where nothing is left (§3 `m = 0`).
4. *Demand.* One available note of exactly the quantity or a pair summing to it, signed by their owners; the presenter
   key is `presentSecret`'s over their tags in input order, the read's witnessed index and the holder's deadline (§8);
   the pool's door and gap rules. `repeats` names earlier demands presenting a selected note, as the pool's.
5. *Presented notes spend as any other; lit has no freshen.* C3.1's selection rule (a payment or burn takes no presented
   note; `freshen` moves one demand's) keeps a pool note's tag from linking two demands. Lit's tags are public functions
   of public commitments and the spend graph is public (§§2, 11), so the rule protects nothing and could strand a
   wallet whose every note an ended demand presented: with a full window (every seed-restored wallet after its first
   read), the move, a payment with change and a freshen (which allocates) would all be refused. Lit selection skips
   only locked and reserved notes; `freshen` is refused (INVALID) for lit.
6. *Accept (§§4, 8).* K's wallet names `acceptSecret(seed, demand, deadline)`'s key and K signs `acceptanceBytes`
   through the `BackerSigner` (`keyedAccept`; pool-v3's `accept` returns its own type), saved once per alias and per
   demand and deadline in the one table, the owner stored as hex. The settled note is found by M14g1's acceptance marks.
7. *Settle.* The acceptance must verify under the obligor, answer this wallet's standing demand (found from the seed by
   its presenter key), be due no later than the demand and not behind the horizon, and leave the demand's nullifiers
   unspent. Refused as `OWN_KEY` where its owner is a key of this wallet that K can see: an owner key of a held backing
   within its window (the demand's inputs name some) or the presenter key of a demand it presents or saved, which no scan marks. The record is
   `(segment, demand, owner)` with `deadline || acceptanceSignature || releaseSignature`, the release by the presenter key
   over `releaseBytes` with this settlement's own statement hash. No disclosure count and no pending-settlement rule
   (§7): the output derives from the demand's nullifiers, so at most one settlement of a demand is admitted.
   Limit: the refusal does not stop K escaping dishonour. K sees a holder's owner keys (a demand's openings name them)
   and can publish, in time, an acceptance naming one; refused, it still reads as answered under C3.8 (the holder's
   lapse), and settled, the notes would come back. The holder withdraws. Whether §7 should read such an acceptance as no
   answer is recorded in WORK.md's Next as a lit-v1 question.
8. *Withdraw and publish.* The presenter key signs the kind-5 statement; a demand, settlement and withdrawal publish as
   lit kinds 1, 3 and 4 and an acceptance as kind 2, routed to the act's backing.
9. *Window move (§8).* `moveWindow`: only once the backing's window is full (its highest exposed or found index `e` is
   `h + 256`), a payment of the smallest available note or pair covering more than the optional fee, wholly less the fee,
   to `e`'s key; no index is allocated (§8's exception) and none past `h + 256` is named. A second move is refused while
   a saved record not failed pays `e`'s key. Once it is final `h = e` and requests resume. "Closes any request that named
   its highest exposed index" is read as: that request's key is never handed out again (an exact retry is refused
   `CLOSED`), while a payer's output to it is still credited, since the move's own output never is (its inputs are all
   the wallet's). One backing per statement (§8 permits several; not taken). The intent binds the backing, the fee and
   `e`; an exact retry matches on backing and fee. A seed-restored wallet syncs before moving (WINDOW otherwise).
10. *Not here.* `presentation` (C3.8's reading; `dishonour.ts` reads pool-v3 records) stays pool-v3's; the service's wire
    and client (M14g3) and the commands (M14g4) are unchanged.

**Alternatives:**
- *A random issue nonce kept in the saved act:* excluded by §8 (K derives it deterministically), and a copy restored from
  the seed, or a second copy, could issue one request twice, both admitted.
- *Keeping C3.1's selection rule for lit, with freshen:* item 5's deadlock, for a privacy rule with nothing to protect.
- *Refusing a payer's output to a closed request:* an honest payment in flight when the window moved would go uncredited,
  and the payee might charge again.
- *Allowing the move whenever `h < e`:* a fee spent before the window needs it, and repeated moves while one is pending.

**Review:** an independent adversarial review of the design draft found three majors and five minors, all resolved. M1:
C3.1's selection filter deadlocks a lit wallet whose notes ended demands presented (item 5's reasoning; filter dropped,
freshen refused). M2: the settle refusal missed the presenter key, whose output no scan marks, and its stated rationale
missed C3.8 (item 7: presenter key added, `OWN_KEY` named, limit recorded). M3: closing a request by refusing credit
loses an honest payment, the move was allowed before the window was full, and duplicate moves paid twice (item 9).
Minors: the nonce guarantees one issuance per key and quantity only, so a saved issue to the key refuses another and an
issue to an own key is refused (item 2); the alternative is excluded by §8 itself; the pool-v3 resolution is
verdict-preserving with the lit settlement condition stated (item 1). Recorded as limits: a lit burn remade under a new
alias after its segment ended takes a new change index (acts are remade, not re-signed); a settlement statement reused
under a second acceptance naming the same owner with another deadline settles only under the first alias saved (the
statement identity excludes the deadline); a seed-restored wallet refuses no acceptance naming a key of a backing it has
not read yet (no note is lost: it is found once read).
An independent adversarial review of the integrated patch found no blocker or major and three minors, each reproduced by
a probe and fixed with a regression test: two issues racing to one key with different quantities both passed a check
made before their reads (it runs again where each saves); a burn's change at `h + 256` did not stop a move paying the
same key (a lit burn's change is now a saved output the move reads); an acceptance naming the presenter key of the
holder's other standing demand stranded its output (every presenter key of a demand this seed presents is refused). Its
nit: a restored backup's move intent was checked for shape only (its record must now pay the moved index's key, and the
fee's, at an exposed index). Its probe of a remade issue caught a first draft of the burn fix that also recorded issue
outputs, which a remade issue repeats by design; issues record none. Its read-back confirmed the fixes and found the burn
fix's own collision: a burn remade after its segment ended frees its note, and a move of that note with a fee equal to the
burn's quantity derives the burn's change exactly (§2: output 0 over the same nullifier), so its output row raised an
unnamed error that poisoned the handle. Burns keep no output rows; the move derives the keys pending burns pay from their
records, with a regression test. It also named two edge gaps in the presenter-key refusal (a demand of another backing, a
saved demand that never stood), closed by adding every saved demand's presenter key.

**Evidence and limits:** `test/lit-wallet.test.ts` (16 tests; M14g2's nine new, a lit journal serving the evidence, no
verifier): K's issue saved, retried, refused for another quantity, another alias, a signer not the obligor's, an own key
(`OWN_KEY`) and an output already admitted; a copy restored from K's seed building the same record; an issue whose nonce
the holder's seed derives credited to no request; a burn with change to the next index and one with none; a demand over a
pair with §8's presenter key, locked notes and the standing demand found; K's acceptance with `acceptSecret`'s owner,
settlement refusing an input owner's key and the presenter key (`OWN_KEY`) and an unsigned acceptance, then admitted, the
backer's wallet holding the settled note; a withdrawal after which the presented note pays a payee, freshen refused, and
lit publication frames; a window move refused before the window is full, at index 256 with a closed request (`CLOSED`),
a second move refused, the payer's output to the closed key credited and requests resuming at 257; a seed-restored wallet
moving its window at 956 and requesting at 957; an issue whose segment ended failed and made again under a new alias with
the same output, admitted and credited once; the review's three cases; a move with a fee over a failed burn's freed note; a backup holding a move restored with the move
and the closed request. Five guards were each removed in turn and a test failed each time. Pool-v3's wallet suites pass unchanged under the unified resolution.
Limits: no gap-route (force) act is exercised for lit; `presentation`, the service wire and the commands are later
milestones; one backing per move.

**Spec change:** none needed (lit-v1 §8 leaves the nonce derivation and the wallet's selection to the wallet).

## 2026-10-06 — Hold lit notes in the one wallet, with owner keys per backing found under a doubling window (slice 14 M14g)

**Status:** accepted after review (spec change below); delivery is in WORK.md.

**Question:** M14f moved lit-v1's adoption after a lit wallet and the commands have exercised §8's keys, requests and
payments. The [lit design](#2026-10-05--make-the-transparent-profile-lit-notes-under-the-pools-rules-with-each-output-named-by-the-statement-that-creates-it-design-slice-next-10)
(item 5) shares the wallet and has each construction supply its holding scan and its proving path. The pool wallet
(`wallet-store.ts`) finds its notes by a witness predicate inside the replay (capsule trial), keeping a mark per own
output in the kept replay file so a read costs what is new (§14); a namespace without a note tree kept no mark. §8 found
lit notes by owner keys `ownerSecret_i` derived from the seed and domain, from index 0 "until 256 consecutive indices
appear in no output", and bounded exposure at `h + 256`. How does the one wallet hold lit notes, find them incrementally,
and take and credit requests?

**Decision:**
1. *One wallet, the construction an option.* `V3Wallet` takes `construction` (pool-v3's by default) as the readers and
   the journal do. Custody, storage, backup, turn order, reads, routing, resolution, submission and publication stay
   one implementation; a construction supplies its holding scan, its request and credit rule, and how a statement is
   authorized (a local prover, or owner signatures). A lit wallet file has its own profile and one more table; pool-v3's
   schema, profile and backup bytes are unchanged, so existing pool wallets open and export as before.
2. *Owner keys per backing (lit-v1 §8 changed).* `ownerSecret = HMAC-SHA256(ownerRoot, backing || u64 i)`. A wallet holds
   many backings, possibly in several scopes or on several venues, and each read and each restoration covers one
   backing's scope; with keys shared across backings, a wallet paid at index 0 in backing A, at 1–600 in B and at 601 in
   A lost the 601 note on restoring A (the rule stops at 256), and the restored wallet then named keys B's requests had
   named. Each backing now has its own indices, window and restoration over its own trails; a backing's name binds its
   venue, so a seed on two venues shares no key either.
3. *A scan under doubling windows.* The replay's witness predicate marks an output whose owner is `ownerSecret`'s key for
   a backing the wallet holds and an index below that backing's window, or a settlement's output whose owner is
   `acceptSecret`'s key over that settlement's own demand and acceptance deadline (§8). A namespace without a note tree
   keeps the mark with no path. A backing's window is the smallest power of two, at least 512, above `h + 256`, where `h`
   is the highest index §8's restoration rule finds in an output of that backing in the read's canonical state (final,
   C2.10.12), spent outputs included; an output of another backing to the key is the wallet's note but moves no index.
   Every window is part of the predicate's identity, so kept state names them. A read whose `h` needs a larger window
   reads again from nothing under it. Then no marked index of the backing lies in `[h + 1, h + 256]` and `h + 256` is
   below the window, so every index the rule finds is below it; a wallet pays at most `log2` of its highest index such
   full replays per backing over its life, and one more each time it first holds a backing.
4. *Exposure.* Per backing, the wallet persists `h` (monotone, from its reads), the highest index any read found (beyond
   a gap too) and its highest exposed index `e`. It allocates indices in order for every output it expects (requests and
   change), never at or below one it exposed or found, persists `e` in the transaction that saves the request or the
   payment naming the key, and refuses (`WINDOW`) an index past `h + 256`. A wallet restored from its seed alone reads
   every index of every backing through `h + 256` as exposed (§8); an encrypted handoff carries the rows, so a restored
   copy never names a key twice.
5. *Requests and credit.* A lit request is the backing, the quantity and the owner key (§8), in an application frame
   beside pool-v3's (`moe/wallet/lit/v1/request`, authenticated by its digest as the pool's is). `fulfill` credits an
   unspent output of the canonical state to that key, of that backing and quantity, unless its statement consumed notes
   all of them the wallet's own (a spend's or burn's inputs, a settlement's demand's notes; an issue consumes none; §8
   changed to say so), and persists the output's commitment as credited to that request alone before reporting it;
   final acceptance is the pool's (canonical finality, current spentness, no standing lock). The mark keeps the consumed
   nullifiers, and crediting checks each against the wallet's marks.
6. *Payments.* A lit payment spends one note or a pair (the pool's selection) into the payee's output, an optional fee
   output and a change output to a fresh index where the inputs exceed the order; no padding, zero input or zero output
   (§11). Each input's owner secret signs the statement; planning, signing and saving run in one turn with no await, so
   the change index is allocated in the saving transaction. A payment whose segment stops being canonical is signed again
   for the canonical one with the same inputs and outputs, which derive from its nullifiers (§2). Its fate reads the
   statement's derived outputs as the pool's reads its four.
7. *Order.* M14g1: items 1–6 for `request`, `sync`, `fulfill`, `prepare`, `submit` and re-signing, with standing demands
   found through `presentSecret`. M14g2: the acts (issue with K's kept nonce, demand, accept refusing an owner that is one
   of the wallet's keys, settle, withdraw, burn, freshen, publish) and §8's window move by a self-payment; until then a
   wallet restored from its seed cannot request (its window is full) and pays only without change. M14g3: the service's
   wire and client per construction. M14g4: `moe` dispatch on the terms' construction.

**Alternatives:**
- *A lit wallet of its own:* custody, backup, reads, resolution and submission would have two implementations.
- *Keys shared across backings, restoring every backing ever used together:* a restorer must know and reach every
  backing the seed ever touched, and one missing scope loses notes and reuses keys.
- *One seed per backing:* every backing a holder takes needs its own backup.
- *Applying §8's rule in one pass in history order* (extending the window as outputs are found): it agrees with the
  whole-history rule only where no output's index is more than 256 past every earlier one.
- *Scanning every output at each read* instead of keeping marks: each read would cost the whole history (§14).
- *A fixed large window:* every handle derives every key in it, and a wallet past it loses outputs.

**Review:** an independent adversarial review of the first draft of this entry (keys shared across backings, `h` the
highest marked index, issues and settlements unconsidered by the credit rule) found one blocker and two majors, all taken:
the per-backing keys (item 2, a spec change); a settlement of the payee's own demand to its request key credited as a
payment (item 5, a spec wording change, and M14g2's acceptance refusal); and an index reused by a race between a request
and a payment's change, or by a database rolled back (item 4's allocation in the saving transaction and above every found
index). Its minors are taken: `h` is what the rule finds, not any marked index (item 3); the milestone order says what a
seed-restored wallet cannot do before M14g2 (item 7); pool-v3's schema is unchanged (item 1). Not taken: crediting only
outputs witnessed after the request's own creation view, which would also blunt a rolled-back database; a database rolled
back past exposed keys remains outside the wallet's custody contract (protected storage, one active copy), as for the pool.
A fresh read-back of the spec change found no blocker and one major, taken in spec 3f62fd9 and the scan: an output of
backing C paid to a key of backing B moved B's `h`, so a wallet could expose B keys a restoration of B never reaches;
only outputs of the backing count. Its minors are taken in the same commit: the allocation rule excepts the window move,
which may move several backings' windows in one statement; "the wallet's own" names notes to keys the seed derives, and
an issue is credited unless the wallet made it (M14g2, where the wallet issues); a settlement's output is derived; `h` is
"found", not "reached"; item 3's argument names `[h + 1, h + 256]`. Recorded, not changed: a note worth no more than the
fee cannot fund a window move (an output is positive).
An independent adversarial review of the M14g1 patch found one major, fixed with a regression test that fails without
it: a kept mark's owner and consumed fields were trusted, so a mark rewritten under a re-recorded digest could credit a
self-payment or raise a backing's `h` (and with it exposure past what a restoration finds). A mark's owner is now checked
against the seed's keys (its backing and index among those derived, or its acceptance's key) when read, and the notes a
statement consumed are read from the replay's own nullifier rows, not the mark. Its minors: a spent payment is refused
`SPENT`, not `ABSENT`; a keyed backup must show no request value or exposure no wallet writes; re-signing checks the
saved record's inputs against its reservations. Recorded as limits: a payer restored from its seed, or another copy, can
pay one request twice (a payer marks no output to another's key; the payee credits one); the predicate's identity names
every held backing's window, so first holding a backing or growing one window replays every held scope once, and the
replaced namespaces stay in the kept file until it is discarded (Next 4 (al)). Its read-back confirmed the owner check and
the consumed rows, and found that withholding a self-payment input's mark (deleting its row, digest re-recorded) still
got the output credited: a deleted row is not seen. The test now also counts as the wallet's own a nullifier a payment or
act saved here reserves, with a regression test that fails without it. Limit: the kept replay file is local custody like
the database (its header says so); a wallet restored from its seed alone, whose kept file is altered so, can still credit
its own earlier self-payment, as an altered database could.

**Evidence and limits:** `test/lit-wallet.test.ts` (M14g1, a lit journal serving the evidence, no verifier): a request
by the backing's index-0 key, exact alias retries and a changed request refused; K's issue credited; a payment with change
to the next index, its exact retry, a refused second order, reservation, a lit receipt and its replay, finality, the payee's
credit once and a second refused; a self-payment to the wallet's own request credited nothing; a payment signed again for
the segment an elective scope change opened, with unchanged outputs, then admitted and credited; an encrypted handoff
continuing at the next index; a seed restoration finding notes at indices 200, 450 and 700 by re-reading under a doubled
window, refusing a request before its first read and after it (window full); `reached` and `windowFor`. Pool-v3's wallet
tests pass unchanged. Limits: the acts, the window move and the payee's view of a demand settled to a request key are
M14g2; the service, its wire and client, and the CLI still read pool-v3 only (M14g3–g4); the full replay a window growth
costs is not measured. The window, the marks and the request frame are the wallet's own.

**Spec change:** lit-v1 §8 (draft), money-from-first-principles branch `claude/lit-v1-backing-keys` (0918e94, 3f62fd9),
stacked on spec PR #15.

## 2026-10-06 — Serve a lit scope from the one operator journal, the construction an option (slice 14 M14f)

**Status:** accepted; review and delivery are in WORK.md.

**Question:** The [direction](#2026-10-05--build-extensions-claim-layer-profiles-beside-the-shielded-pool-each-chosen-per-backing)
makes each claim-layer profile a complete capability through the `moe` commands, and the
[design](#2026-10-05--make-the-transparent-profile-lit-notes-under-the-pools-rules-with-each-output-named-by-the-statement-that-creates-it-design-slice-next-10)
(item 5) shares the journal's admission and commitment schedule, the receipt clocks and the evidence and replay stores.
M14c–M14e put lit records through the one state machine and the one reader; the operator journal (`store.ts`) still read
and wrote pool-v3's frames only. How does an operator serve a lit scope, and when is `moe/lit/v1` adopted?

**Decision:**
1. *One journal, the construction an option.* `V3OperatorJournal` takes `construction` (pool-v3's by default) and reads
   every frame through it: domain, terms, segment header and identity, scope root (none for lit), snapshot, receipt,
   package, trail parts and configuration item. A construction without proofs takes no verifier (`ownVerifier`, as the
   readers do). The journal's identity row names the construction's domain, so a file serves one construction, and one
   opened as the other is refused as storage.
2. *What the journal writes joins the construction seam.* `Construction.journal` holds a record's canonical bytes and
   statement identity, the configuration item and the receipt record: pool-v3's receipt names its scope root and the
   evidence triple, lit-v1's neither (lit-v1 §5). The audit reads a stored receipt through the readers' receipt view,
   authenticated under the header of the segment the receipt names, which the journal serves.
3. *Reopening rebuilds a lit journal's outputs.* Reopening re-verifies nothing and checks that the stored state reproduces
   the last signed snapshot and its own tip. No chain value covers lit's output set (lit-v1 §10), so a construction without
   a note tree also rebuilds its output rows through the tip, and each import's, from the trails the journal serves, once
   per process (`storedTipHolds` with the trails).
4. *Adoption comes after the journal and the wallet.* Slice 14 adopts `moe/lit/v1` once a lit wallet and the commands have
   exercised §8's keys, requests and payments, as pool-v3's adoption came last, once nothing left could move a byte
   ([order](2026-09.md#2026-09-29--order-configuration-adoption-and-state-its-proving-parameters)). No backing can declare
   lit before a release, so the order delays no deployment.

**Alternatives:**
- *A lit journal of its own:* it would duplicate the scheduling, fencing, venue answers, recovery, rescoping and serving
  the design placed in the shared journal, so every rule would have two implementations.
- *Trusting a lit journal's output rows on reopening,* as the pool trusts the rows its roots do not reach: the pool's note
  root checks its output set at every snapshot, and lit has no such value, so a changed output row would let a forged
  input pass admission (the reader's M14e M2 for the journal). The rebuild decodes each record once per process start
  and hashes its outputs; its cost at depth is not measured.
- *Adopting first* and building the journal and wallet against adopted bytes: a byte change the wallet needs would then
  be `moe/lit/v2`.

**Evidence and limits:** `test/lit-journal.test.ts`: every statement kind admitted with lit receipts and served to the lit
reader at the same position, totals and chains; exact replay; refusals by check (malformed bytes, a pool-v3 record, K's
and an owner's signature, arithmetic, an input no statement created, another segment, a request's kind, a spent note);
a file of the other construction refused; reopening with outputs rebuilt and a changed output row refused, including one
admitted after the last commitment (that case passed before the tip rebuild); an elective scope change importing the
journal's own state, an imported note spent and refused again after reopening, and an audit; a return after silence
adopting a forced lit demand. The pool-v3 journal tests pass unchanged. Limits: the HTTP service, its wire and client
read pool-v3 records and receipts only, and the CLI opens pool-v3 journals only (M14g); no venue drill of a lit journal; the
rebuild's cost at depth.

A fresh adversarial review of the patch found pool-v3 unchanged (receipt bytes, encode and identity, the verifier's binding,
the own package, the reopen checks, and the audit's receipt check in every reachable state), lit receipts exactly §5's and
final under the lit reader, the constructions isolated (identity, terms, records, takeover evidence), and lit reopening sound
during a pending return, after an adopted forced demand and after a takeover. It found one blocker, now fixed with a
regression test: the journal copies each imported namespace without import rows of its own (`copyFrontier`), so rebuilding
a copy whose settlement ends a demand an earlier segment stood up derived no output, and an honest journal refused to
reopen after a third segment imported both; the rebuild now reads such a demand from the importer's closure. Two minors are
fixed with tests: a trail record that no longer chains, read on reopening, is now `STORAGE` rather than an unnamed
evidence refusal, and an own output row past the tip, which would become visible and spendable at the next admission, now
fails reopening. Its read-back confirmed the three fixes, found no reachable demand the closure misses (a takeover's
import of an import run end to end, a multi-parent merge reasoned), and one further minor, now fixed with a test: the rebuild
trusted stored demand rows, from which a settlement takes its output and nullifiers, so a raised quantity minted at the next
settlement; each demand the trail stands up must now equal its row. That also closes, for readers' kept state, the demand
residual M14e recorded; its chain-value residual stands, as do demand and nullifier rows placed past the tip (harmless
without K's acceptance of a forged demand) and the full own-output scan on each rebuild (an index if it matters). Not taken: a lit journal ignores a supplied verifier and takes any `Construction` object, as the readers do
(M14d). The review also noted, outside this patch, that reopening a journal of either construction does not find a deleted
nullifier row, which surfaces later as a generic error from the spent tree (Next 4 (af)).

**Spec change:** none.

## 2026-10-06 — Exclude a lit record that splits but does not decode, rebuild kept imports, and vector every layout (slice 14 M14e)

**Status:** accepted (specification change and integrated patch reviewed). Adopting `moe/lit/v1` follows.

**Question:** The [conformance read](archive/2026-10-06-lit-v1-conformance-read.md) of lit-v1 held adoption on one
blocker and two majors. B1: §3 says the length fields "let a reader split a committed record without decoding it (§5)",
and §5 hashes the authorization "whatever its length", but §6 applied pool-v3 §10.1, whose authentication covers records
"that can be decoded under §5" and is inconclusive for the rest; a record with exact lengths whose statement fails §3 left
its checkpoint unresolved. M2: §10 rebuilds "the output set from the kept statements and imports"; the reader rebuilt a
kept namespace's own outputs only. M1: the vectors bound records, publications and one owner key.

**Decision:**
1. **A served lit trail authenticates every record that splits** (lit-v1 §6). §10.1's procedure and §12.1's
   served-trail match cover records that split, where pool-v3's cover records that decode. A record that splits but whose
   statement does not decode under §3, or whose authorization is not §3's length for its kind, fails replay at its position
   (`MALFORMED`, admission's code), as a committed kind-7 record does, so its checkpoint can be excluded on its complete
   trail (C2.10.11). A record that does not split stays inconclusive. 8200 bytes bounds the record; a field past the fault
   evidence's 4096 bytes splits and fails replay, but no fault evidence carries it. Code: lit's `digests` from
   `splitRecord`; `judgmentOf` turns the decode's `EncodingError` alone into the refusal. Pool-v3 never reaches it: its
   evidence triple needs the decode, and admission decodes first.
2. **Kept lit state rebuilds its imports** (§10). `keptStateHolds` also requires each imported namespace's own outputs
   through the position imported to be its segment's trail's, cut at the chain value kept there (`TrailEvidence.trail`);
   a namespace this process replayed or rebuilt reads nothing. A missing trail or mismatch is kept state to discard.
3. **Vectors bind every layout** (`test/fixtures/lit-v1-vectors.json`): history and evidence chains over a valid
   four-statement prefix, snapshot, receipt message and record, acceptance and release, header, terms with every clause,
   their name and K's signature, trail, fault evidence, package, and the settlement and presenter secrets.

**Rationale:**
- *Keeping pool-v3's inconclusive reading* and rewording §3 and §5 would keep a stall a buggy operator causes: an unresolved
  carrying checkpoint leaves silence lapse unresolved (C2.10.11), so reads wait for a term to end. Lit's pair needs no
  decode, so the split costs nothing and a fault becomes an exclusion. Attribution holds: the chain must reproduce the
  operator's signed snapshot, so substituted bytes cannot exclude a checkpoint.
- *Imports resting on §14's file digest*, as M14d recorded, falls short of §10, which rebuilds the output set "from the
  kept statements and imports": a pool spend proves membership under an accepted root, but a lit input is live by the
  output set alone, and no root in a lit snapshot checks an imported output. The check reuses the own-output rebuild and
  runs once per namespace per process. Like the pool's root checks, it guards against kept rows the digest cannot tell
  apart (damage under a digest recorded for it, state written by faulty code); §14's model keeps the digest in storage
  only the reader writes, and the rows the rebuild itself reads stay vouched for by it.
- *Costs:* one sentence in §6 and one in §12; a catch around one decode; one interface method; kept lit state discarded
  once (the pin moved).
- *Falsifiers:* two conforming lit decoders disagreeing on a split record (valid in one reader, excluded in another), which
  the vectors and hostile tests must pin; a pool-v3 verdict, refusal code or report verdict that changes.

**Evidence:** `test/lit-reader.test.ts`: six records that split but do not decode (a value 0, a small-order key, kind 8, a
63-byte authorization, both lengths zero, a 5000-byte statement) excluded by `MALFORMED` with the last valid checkpoint
canonical; a record that does not split unresolved; an opening holding one excluded `OPENING`; a trail replacing the last
valid prefix's record excluded `CONTINUITY`; a forged imported output row (digest re-recorded) refused `INPUT` after a
discard where it had been admitted and refused only at `SNAPSHOT`, and an honest kept successor resumed without one; one
operator key committing a pool checkpoint between lit ones passed as non-carrying by its directory, a receipt after it
invalid and after the lit one final (§6); demand publications summing past a `u64` or naming two backings without force or
check. The B1 and M2 tests fail on the parent's code. The unit suite passed at the M2 commit (1262 tests).
A fresh review of the specification change agreed with the choice against pool-fault C2.10.10–11 and pool-v3 §§7.1,
9.1, 12.1 and 14 (it fuzzed 20,000 mutated vector records: decode and re-encode were byte-exact and the two digest paths
agreed); its major (§12.1 unnamed) and minors (the per-kind length, the field bound, §12's listing) and its read-back's two
wording fixes (the 4096-byte bound's section, the lapse qualifier) were taken.
A fresh adversarial review of the integrated patch found the pool-v3 paths unchanged (every authenticated pool record
decodes; admission and adoption decode first; `keptImportsHold` returns at once for a construction with a note tree),
B1's attribution held (`MALFORMED` only on records chained to the signed snapshot), and honest two-level imports resumed
without a discard while a rewritten grandparent row was caught. Its two majors assume the digest itself rewritten, outside
§14's model, and are recorded as residuals rather than closed: the import's chain value is read from its kept event row,
so a rewrite that also redirects it to a fork trail the package carries passes; and kept demand rows, own (as M14d
recorded) and imported, are not rebuilt, so a rewritten quantity mints at a later settlement, where the pool's proof
would fail. Closing either would anchor the chain value to the import's authenticated snapshot and compare the demands the
trail stands up with the rows. Its minors: the segment is now read from the import row's key; `resumable`'s other
candidate namespaces check own outputs only (also digest-vouched); a lit record failing to decode at an adopted position
reports `MALFORMED` before `ADOPTION`, as a request reports `KIND`; an evidence file kept before this change holds lit
trails cut at the first undecodable record, unresolved until supplied again (lit is a draft, no deployment holds one).

**Spec change:** [lit-v1 §§6, 12 at 7e1ddd5](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/blob/7e1ddd5/lit-v1.md#6-header-fault-evidence-trail-and-package).

## 2026-10-06 — Read lit packages through the one package reader and walk, the construction a read option (slice 14 M14d)

**Status:** accepted (integrated patch reviewed). Adopting `moe/lit/v1` (Construction C0a) follows.

**Question:** M14c judges lit records in the one state machine. The package reader and the walk around it
(`package-reader.ts`, `scope-reader.ts`, `reader.ts`, the evidence store, the receipt walk, the non-service count and
the fault observer) read pool-v3's objects directly: its configuration and verifier, scope root, header, trail and
package contexts, snapshot and receipt frames, terms codec, publications, requests and fault evidence. How does a lit
package reach the same C2.10 and C2b rules?

**Decision:**
1. **One walk over each construction's frames.** A construction's adapter carries `reader` frames
   (`pool/v3/construction.ts` `ReaderFrames`): its specification revision, configuration hash and bytes check, verifier
   identities, whether its records carry proofs, its scope root, header, trail, package and terms codecs, snapshot
   frame, a receipt view (fields, signature check under the segment's authority, event match), record digests, a
   publication view, a request view (C2b.5.2) and fault-evidence targets (§9 observations, each marked where §9.1 lets
   it exclude). Every walk rule stays once; only object reads go through the frames.
2. **The construction is a read option.** `readPackage` and `readFrontier` take `construction` (pool-v3's by default):
   the selection must name its configuration (`CONFIGURATION`, in pool-v3's check order), and a frontier's signed terms
   decode under its codec. The fault observer, kept-state checks (`keptStateHolds`, `storedTipHolds`) and `ReplayResult`
   take it explicitly and refuse a namespace of another construction.
3. **No verifier for lit.** `ReadOptions` makes the verifier optional; a construction without proofs reads under a
   verifier naming no circuit, whose call is a programming failure, and ignores one given. Kept contexts and replay
   identities name the construction's specification revision (`pool-v3 e7f7f24`, `lit-v1 1bf5bfc`).
4. **One construction per evidence store.** `EvidenceStore` is bound to one; a party's file records it
   (`evidence_construction`, beside layout 4, which is unchanged) and refuses another's (TypeError); a file made before
   the row that holds evidence is pool-v3's.
5. **Lit's readings, named in its frames:** no scope root (`CanonicalCheckpoint.scope` undefined; receipts carry none,
   §5); a request counts where its owner's signature verifies and its commitment is an output of the canonical state,
   its tag unspent and unlocked (§7); §6's compact intrinsic failures are named per check (`intrinsicFailures`:
   `ARITHMETIC`, then each failing owner's `SIGNATURE` in input order, or K's for an issue), facts carrying no proof
   digest; a withdrawal's or settlement's signature fact is not reported (it needs the demand, which is state). A
   frontier read asking for `answers` (pool-v3's C3.5 disclosure count and C3.8 releases) is refused for lit
   (TypeError): the lit wallet's reads are a later slice. Results are generic in the forced record type (`R = Record`;
   a lit read names `LitRecord`).
6. **Kept lit state rebuilds its outputs (lit-v1 §10).** No root in a lit snapshot checks the output set, so before a
   kept lit namespace is resumed or its kept class reused, its own output rows through the position must be exactly, in
   order, the outputs the class's served trail derives (`keptOutputsHold`; a settlement reads its demand from the trail's
   earlier records, an imported one from the state before it). A mismatch is kept state to discard (§14), and the read
   classifies again. Imported outputs are their own namespaces', checked where those classes are reused. Pool-v3 keeps
   its note-root check. The rebuild is incremental: per store a process holds, each namespace is known through the
   position this process's replay wrote or a rebuild checked (with its own outputs and the demands its trail stood up),
   and only records past that are derived and only rows past its leaves read (`ReplayStore.ownOutputs`). The caller has
   matched the trail's chain at the position to the namespace's, so a known namespace consults no trail again.

**Rationale:**
- *A lit walk beside the pool's* would hold C2.10's classification, imports, receipts, force and clocks twice, the
  branch lit-v1 §12's falsifier names. *Selecting the construction by the selection's domain through a registry* reads
  more simply, but pool-v3's modules then import lit (test/neutral-core's layering rule); an explicit option keeps the
  pool's code ignorant of lit and the caller's choice visible. *An evidence store detecting each package's construction
  by its context* would need per-row construction tags in every lookup; one store per construction needs one row.
- *Costs:* one frames object per construction and a parameter through the walk; kept state is discarded once (the
  reader rules changed); every retained report binding the changed sources is re-recorded with unchanged verdicts.
- *Falsifiers:* a pool-v3 verdict, refusal code or report verdict that changes; a C2.10 or C2b rule needing a lit branch
  in the walk beyond the frames.

**Evidence:** `test/lit-reader.test.ts` (9 tests) reads lit packages end to end with no verifier: an issue and a payment
with derived outputs and totals; six hostile trails excluded by code (`SIGNATURE` for an owner and for K, `ARITHMETIC`,
`INPUT`, `KIND` for a request, `SPENT`) with the last valid checkpoint canonical; misstated supply and history
(`SNAPSHOT`); a successor importing its predecessor and spending its note; a receipt final and a forged one refused; a
withheld checkpoint excluded by compact fault evidence (`SIGNATURE`, `ARITHMETIC`) with its facts; a demand forced past
the silence duration beside a forged one refused; requests counted only for the owner's signature over a live note;
cross-construction stores, files, selections and terms refused; a kept lit namespace holding against its own trail and
not another's of as many records (with a settlement's derived output), and a kept file resumed without a discard
(failing the rebuild fails both). Writing it found one defect, fixed: lit's request check called a helper that verifies
only kinds 1 and 5. The unit suite (1260 tests), `npm run check` and `check:pool:local-replay` (223 groups, 65 real
proofs) pass; pool-v3's tests pass unchanged but for two structural ones (the specification pin now read from the
construction; two call sites).

One fresh adversarial review of the integrated patch traced pool-v3's paths line by line against the parent (fault facts,
proof caching and verifier-error wrapping, request counting, receipts with the scope root, force, the evidence chain, both
reads' check order, the rules' bytes) and found them equivalent, lit-v1's readings held, and isolation across stores,
namespaces and kept contexts. Its blocker was a script passing a stray fourth argument to `faultObserver`, now the
construction (`scope-check.mjs`, as `fault-check.mjs` was), which would have failed the real-proof replay group: fixed,
and the observer now names a non-construction argument. Its major was item 6's gap (no output check on resumption), taken
in code rather than by amending the reviewed §10 residual. Its minors were taken (the construction row read inside the
busy-file mapping, the stale rule row) or recorded: a host's database (the journal) records no construction, and
`ReadOptions` leaves a pool read's verifier optional at compile time (a runtime TypeError).

A fresh read-back of the rebuild found it sound (no path reuses a kept lit class without it; honest kept state with a
settlement of an imported demand, an adopted block and position 0 holds) and one major: it derived the whole segment
prefix at every checkpoint, including namespaces the read itself had just replayed, so a lit read grew with records
times checkpoints (measured 18.0 s → 36.7 s for 600 chained spends over 12 checkpoints). Taken: the incremental rebuild
above. The same shape now reads in 17.1 s, as with the check disabled. Recorded: a tampered demand row in the namespace
itself is not detected (the trail's demand record is read instead), and an imported namespace's rows are checked only
where its class is reused in the read, as pool-v3's rows beyond its note root rest on §14's file digest.

**Spec change:** none (lit-v1 at `1bf5bfc` read as written).

## 2026-10-06 — Run CI's real-proof checks in parallel groups and skip them only for unread changes (tooling)

**Status:** accepted (CI tooling; no runtime, report or specification change).

**Question:** CI's pool-v3 job ran fourteen real-proof scripts one after another. On `main` it had grown from about
3 minutes (2026-09-09) to 39–43 on Ubuntu and 43–51 on Windows (2026-10-04 to 10-06) against a 60-minute limit, and a
slice usually waits for it twice: once for its code, and again for a commit holding only the reports re-recorded from
that run and the handoff. How can runs wait less without dropping or weakening a check?

**Decision:**
1. *One list, parallel groups.* `scripts/pool/v3/real-proof.mjs` lists every real-proof script in the old serial
   order with its group and the reports it writes. `npm run check:pool:v3` runs them all in order, as before; CI runs
   one job per group on each system, its matrix taken from that list. Five groups balanced by the serial job's times
   (run 37424183597, Ubuntu): the command drill (8.6 min); local replay with Ergo (7.8); both redemption checks (6.9);
   conformance, journal and history (5.7); the six other stores and the budget check (2.7).
2. *Reports stay per job.* Each job copies each report its scripts wrote to an upload directory at its repository
   path, refusing one missing or not written since its script started; a final job, only when every group passed on
   both systems, merges them into `pool-v3-reports-<os>` (the old name and layout, so runs re-record as before) and
   checks all twelve arrived.
3. *Shared parameters, own build.* `actions/cache` keeps `scratch/private-payment-crs` keyed on `parameters.ts` and
   `parameter-files.ts`; `prepare-crs.mjs` still checks every cached file's length and SHA-256. Each job builds `dist`
   itself: `tsc` takes seconds, while sharing a build would add a serial job before every group.
4. *Skip only unread changes.* That final job also records the tested tree (`git ls-tree -r -z` of the checkout, for
   a pull request GitHub's merge commit) as the run's `real-proof-tree` artifact. A gate job
   (`scripts/real-proof-gate.mjs`) compares the tree under test with the records of the ten nearest ancestors of the
   pushed commit (a pull request's head) that have one, made by runs of this repository, and skips the real-proof
   jobs only when one recorded tree differs solely in `docs/*-verification.json` reports, `docs/*.md`,
   `decisions/**/*.md`, AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, DECISIONS.md or WORK.md, each a regular file. README.md and LICENSE are
   packed into the install the command drill runs, so they count as sources. Anything else, a manual dispatch or any
   error runs every group. `check` runs on every push and now also prints `check:evidence`.

**Rationale:**
- *Comparing trees, not commits*, makes the skip sound whichever passing record it finds: the code that passed is
  byte-for-byte the code under test. It also covers a pull request's merge commit (whose base can move) and the push
  after a merge, whose tree a pull request run already tested.
- *Splitting the command drill or local replay by index* was not simple: each builds its proof state up front and
  writes one report, so a split would rebuild that state per part and merge reports. Kept whole; they now set the
  floor (below).
- *One job per script* (28 jobs) would mostly add runner setup and queueing against the account's concurrent-job
  limit; five groups keep 14 jobs in flight with the floor unchanged.
- *A path-filter action or `paths-ignore`* compares with the previous push, not with the last passing tree, so a
  report-only commit after a failed or cancelled run would skip unproved code. Rejected.

**Evidence:** pull request #119. Run 37432108169 (all groups, the change's first run): 12.2 minutes wall-clock against
43.5 for `main`'s serial run 37424183597; the slowest jobs were the command drill (10.6 min on Ubuntu, 11.2 on
Windows, script time) and local replay (9.7, 10.7); every other group finished within 9 minutes. Its merged artifacts
held all twelve reports at the old layout, and the twelve current reports were re-recorded from it. The commit
holding only those reports and the decision skipped the real-proof jobs (run 37433907117, 5.9 minutes, the gate naming
the pass at `49e2f24` and the fifteen unread files); the next, changing only comments in the workflow and
`real-proof.mjs`, ran every group (run 37434740817, 12.5 minutes). `test/real-proof-gate.test.ts` pins the serial list, the twelve reports, and the gate's verdicts on sixteen kinds of
change that must run (sources, scripts, the workflow, README.md, LICENSE, package.json, a mode change, a symlink, a
move out of `src/`, a report beside a source change, among others).

**Limits:** a skipped run proves nothing about the runner image or the latest Node 24 since the recorded pass; a
manual dispatch reruns everything. The gate trusts records made by this repository's runs (writers can already edit
the workflow); a fork's runs are ignored. A recorded tree that has expired (90 days) or lies beyond the newest 100
records is not found, and everything runs.

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-06 — Drill the moe commands live on the testnet, and keep the testnet context without a difficulty floor (slice 10 M10d)

**Status:** accepted (slice 10 M10d, the last milestone of slice 10; drill tooling and documentation, no runtime or
specification change).

**Question:** slice 10's acceptance asked for the `moe` commands' drill (issue → pay → receive → fulfill → demand →
accept → settle → burn, and an offline operator's return) live on the testnet as well as on the synthetic node; only
the machine with the own nodes and the retained testnet wallet can run it. Three local-machine questions came with it:
M10c2's review left the testnet reference context without a difficulty floor ("a reader whose own nodes serve a
difficulty-1 chain under a venue file relabelled as testnet reads it without the synthetic note"; M10d to revisit);
M9c2 (a)'s testnet anchor bound rested on sampled mainnet headers; and the first live run's readback report
(`testnet-reader-check.mjs`) binds sources retired since `2fd0f08`.

**Decision:**
1. *One drill, two chains.* `command-drill.mjs` takes `--testnet --authorized-testnet` and runs the same checks over
   the own testnet node from the same packed install. Where the synthetic drill mines, the live drill waits for
   blocks; a wait round is one block, allowed depth + 2 times as many rounds. `--synthetic` is the hostile switch live
   (venue create, open and terms add refuse it), the terms' silence is 30 blocks (16 synthetic) for real block
   times, commands poll every 5 s, each funding directory's spend budget is 0.5 tERG (serve commits for hours), and
   the bulk issue is two notes: the old 67-statement ceiling is a property of one package in memory, which the
   synthetic drill keeps, not of the chain. Live, it writes `docs/pool-v3-command-testnet-verification.json`, bound
   to its source closure (the drill, the live harness, the `moe` bin's sources, the lockfile).
2. *Funding outside the product.* Each funding directory (`operator init`, `relay init`) makes its own key, as M10b
   decided; the drill pays each key from the retained testnet wallet and, at the end or after a failure, sweeps every
   key back. `testnet.mjs`'s `testnetTransfer` builds the publisher's plain-box layout and signs with the same
   proveDlog, each proof checked by the runtime's `verifyErgoProof` before anything is sent; the node validates again.
3. *No testnet difficulty floor.* A full scan of the testnet's 582,246 headers from 1,025 finds its difficulty fell to
   1 for 7,296 headers in four runs (132,225–134,528; 198,273–200,576; 418,049–420,608; 423,297–459,008), so any floor
   refuses real testnet anchors in such a stretch. The testnet label rests on the reader's own nodes (`venue create`
   asks `/info` for the network), never on work: the testnet's work assures nothing either. The M10c2 note stands as
   documentation, not a finding.
4. *M9c2 (a)'s bound, measured.* A full scan of mainnet from 1,025 to 1,888,276 (1,887,252 headers) finds the least
   difficulty at 1,025 itself, equal to `initialDifficultyHex` (1,199,990,374,400); the testnet context's rule is
   strictly below it, so no mainnet header in that range can anchor a testnet profile.
5. *The readback retires.* Re-recording it would read the first live run's bundle (package format of `acc1ab7`) with
   today's reader; this drill is the current live evidence. `testnet-reader-check.mjs` and its report are cited at
   `2fd0f08` (their removal waits in WORK.md's deletion list); the original live report stays as history.
6. *No 10⁶ design-point run now.* Next 5 keeps it as a lever only if a budget fails; every budget holds to 10⁵.

**Rationale:**
- *A second, live-only script* would repeat the drill's process harness (moe runner, serve, waits, refusal parsing)
  and its checks would drift from the synthetic ones; one script keeps one list of checks with the chain as a
  parameter. Rejected.
- *Funding:* writing the wallet's key into every funding directory would make three publishers compete for one key's
  boxes and copy the retained wallet's secret into drill scratch; the node has no wallet (and importing one needs a
  mnemonic); a product transfer or sweep command would be a new custody surface for drills alone. A collector for the
  location boxes' value (the venue creator's `location.key`) may justify one later (Next 9 candidate, not taken).
- *A floor above 1* refuses the real testnet (above); a floor between 1 and the testnet's usual difficulty does too;
  only a checkpointed testnet header could name the network, and checkpoints were left out on 2026-09-27.

**Evidence:**
- Scans (this entry is their home): every header's `difficulty` as the own nodes' `/blocks/chainSlice` states it,
  mainnet 2026-10-05 (snapshot node, full header chain), testnet 2026-10-06 (archive node).
- Live run (2026-10-06, own testnet node 6.0.6, Node 24.21.0, [report](../docs/pool-v3-command-testnet-verification.json)):
  all 17 checks in 156 minutes over heights 583,356–583,563 (depth 2, silence 30), 227 command processes, the largest
  peak RSS 347 MB (`wallet demand`). Four keys were paid 1 tERG each; the directories reserved 0.0339 tERG for their
  publications; each key was swept back in one round, confirmed at the lag and read back empty. The run cost the
  wallet about 0.043 tERG with the transfers' fees. An earlier attempt stopped after five checks on a same-answer read
  (Review, below); its one funded key was swept by the failure path and read back empty.
- Synthetic drill after the refactor: all 17 checks pass on Windows (Node 24.21.0). Under the PC's Node 24.6.0 a
  command crashed at exit in libuv (`!(handle->flags & UV_HANDLE_CLOSING)`, `src\win\async.c`, 0xC0000409); CI's
  current Node 24 runs the drill on Windows, so the local runs use an official 24.21.0 zip (checksum from nodejs.org's
  SHASUMS256) kept in scratch.
- Review: one fresh reviewer read the patch before the live run: the byte layout and proveDlog are identical to
  `ergo-publisher.ts` (replayed on `MempoolNode`), and the synthetic mode is unchanged. Taken: a sweep that let the
  scratch, keys included, be deleted while it was only in the mempool or listed through a lagging index (blocker: it
  now waits for the index, confirms each sweep at the lag and sweeps again until only dust is left, and only then is
  scratch deleted); the synthetic drill's same-answer reads assume a still chain (the first live run failed there,
  `judgingIndex` 25 against 26: live, a pair is read again until both judge at one index, or compared without it where
  serve has stopped); the report now binds `package.json`, `rss-hook.mjs` and the consumer install's lockfile hash;
  serve is awaited before a failure-path sweep; reservations are labelled as such; boxes above the build height are
  left out; every transfer is replayed offline before it is sent, and `testnet-budget.mjs --check` (CI) checks pay,
  chained pay, sweep and the floors. Left: the record boxes' minimum values stay at the venue's location trees (spent).

**Limits:** one own testnet node at depth 2; the testnet's work and finality assure nothing about mainnet; silence 30
blocks; holders' submissions on loopback (Next 2); no power loss or malicious node; the record boxes' value stays at
the location trees (not swept).

**Spec change:** none needed.

## 2026-10-05 — Judge lit records in the one state machine through a construction view (slice 14 M14c)

**Status:** accepted (design and integrated patch reviewed). The reader, package and scope readers over lit are M14d.

**Question:** slice 14's acceptance replays a lit trail through the shared seams with lit-v1 §7's verdicts. `state.ts`
is pool-v3's one moded state machine (admission, replay, adoption, force); it read v3 records directly (public inputs
by index, field-element keys, anchors in a Poseidon2 note tree, the evidence triple, v3's history hash). How does a lit
record reach the same rules?

**Decision:**
1. **One machine, a construction view.** `state.ts` reads every record through its construction's adapter
   (`pool/v3/construction.ts`: `POOL_V3`; `lit/construction.ts`: `LIT`): the codec, a neutral statement view, the
   chains (genesis, history, evidence) and the note-tree capacity. The view names the context (domain, segment, and
   pool-v3's scope root, which lit has none of), the backings the record names, the demand it ends and whether it reads
   it to be judged at all, the nullifiers spent with their tags aligned, the outputs created, the inputs it reads
   (pool-v3's anchors, `ANCHOR`; lit's input commitments, **`INPUT`**), the demand it stands up (lit's with its input
   nullifiers), the statement check in the proof's place (pool-v3: the proof; lit: **`ARITHMETIC`**, then the owners'
   `SIGNATURE`s) and the issue, withdrawal and settlement signatures over each construction's messages. Every mode
   rule, ordering, continuity, revocation, supply, C3.7 lock and uniqueness check stays once, in `state.ts` and
   `recovery.ts`.
2. **Building a view never throws on a decoded record.** A lit demand's sum past a u64 and mixed backings are left
   for `ARITHMETIC`; a lit settlement or withdrawal whose demand does not stand is refused `DEMAND` before the
   statement check, in every mode including adoption. Its nullifiers and its one output come from the stored demand
   (lit-v1 §3), so v3's `QUANTITY` and `TAGS` checks hold for it without a branch.
3. **Tags are the view's.** Locks, spent-tag rows and C2.10.6's ordering keys read the construction's tags (lit's
   SHA-256 tag, pool-v3's Poseidon2 one), never pool-v3's tag function on a lit nullifier.
4. **Force reads inputs in the snapshot only** (lit-v1 §7's last paragraph): `ForceState.isInput` reads its base, as
   `hasAnchor` does; an output forced since is no input.
5. **Store.** Nullifier, commitment and tag keys are 32-byte big-endian 256-bit values, byte-identical for field
   elements; the note root stays a field element. A namespace records its construction and whether it keeps a note
   tree (`namespace_construction`); a lit namespace keeps outputs in scan order and no tree, anchor or witness, has no
   2^32 capacity, and imports only its own construction. A lit demand's nullifiers are kept beside it
   (`demand_nullifier`); pool-v3 writes neither new table's demand rows. Replay files' layout moves to 11 (kept files
   rebuild), and the journal's database profile to `pool-store/v3/7` (earlier journals refuse to open, as at every
   profile change; none is live).
6. **Check order** (refusal codes are the observable verdict; pool-v3's is unchanged): `KIND`, position `CAPACITY`,
   `ADOPTION` or `CONTEXT` and `SCOPE`, `BACKING` (terms held: the demand's backing where the record reads one, else
   each named), `DEMAND` (where read), `REPEATED_STATEMENT`, evidence `CONTINUITY`, `REVOKED`, the statement check
   (`PROOF` | `ARITHMETIC`, `SIGNATURE`), `BACKING` (each named backing scoped; a lit spend is not exempt), K's
   `SIGNATURE` and `SUPPLY`, `ANCHOR` | `INPUT`, `checkRecovery` (`REPEATED_STATEMENT`, `TAGS`, `LOCKED`, `DEADLINE`,
   `DEMAND`, `SIGNATURE`, `QUANTITY`), `SPENT`, `OUTPUT`, tree `CAPACITY` (pool-v3 only), history `CONTINUITY`. A lit
   demand's notes are unspent through their tags (`LOCKED`), as pool-v3's are.

**Rationale:**
- *A lit machine beside v3's* would copy the mode table, continuity, revocation, supply and C3.7's lock rules: a C3
  change would be made twice, the branch lit-v1's falsifier names. *Synthesizing v3 records from lit statements* would
  reinterpret v3's layout (lit keys are not field elements; a lit spend names two backings).
- *Keeping the demand's record* in place of its nullifiers would decode it at every settlement; the nullifiers are
  fixed by the demand's identity and serve a later settlement scan. *Columns on v3's tables* in place of side tables
  would rewrite pool-v3's rows' layout; the side tables leave them as they were.
- *Costs:* one view object per record; a demand read makes one more indexed query; the 256-bit key read drops the
  field check on reads of kept nf/cm/tag rows (kept files are digest-checked, §14; Poseidon2 and the prover still
  refuse a non-field value); a lit event stores an empty proof digest and the empty note root. Every retained report
  binding `state.ts`, `recovery.ts` or `replay-store.ts` is re-recorded with unchanged verdicts.
- *Falsifiers:* a pool-v3 verdict, refusal code or report verdict that changes; a §7 rule needing a branch in the
  machine beyond the view's fields.

**Evidence:** `test/lit-state.test.ts` (10 tests) replays honest and hostile lit trails in replay, admission, adoption
and force, with derived outputs and both chains recomputed by an independent node:crypto oracle (the spent root by
the shared pool-spent set, whose contexts lit keeps): every refusal by its
code (context, kind, unscoped and two-backing spends, inputs never created, duplicate inputs, unbalanced spends, mixed
backings and a sum past a u64, owner, K, presenter, acceptance and release signatures, supply, a repeated statement,
an issuance signed again for a successor, a spent input, locks, a spent tag, deadlines and the door, a settlement or
withdrawal without its demand, an output forced since the snapshot), a lock on a note whose nullifier and tag exceed
the field, a second or repeated settlement, swapped owner signatures, a lock imported from the predecessor, a scope
root named for a lit segment, a forced spend, a stranger's forced withdrawal and a late forced settlement (a refused
force leaves the overlay as it was), and cross-construction imports and handles refused. Mutations of the lit view (inputs, arithmetic, owner
signatures, demand nullifiers, settlement tags and output, force's input base, the store's tree flag) each fail it; one
survivor (output backings in the scoped list) is redundant, since an output's backing must be an input's. The full unit
suite (1221 tests) passes unchanged; CI's real-proof jobs re-run pool-v3 replay.

One fresh adversarial review of the design found no blocker and four majors, all taken (items 2–5: tags from the
view, a view that never throws, force's input base, the journal's profile), with minors taken: both `BACKING` slots
kept so v3's order holds, every remaining v3-fixed path (genesis, `settledFor`, `scanOutput`, `checkTaken`,
`copyFrontier`) routed through the adapter and a handle refusing another construction's namespace, a range-checked
key encoder, a one-input lit demand's zero second tag, the re-recorded reports stated, and the acceptance cases above.

A fresh adversarial review of the integrated patch found one blocker: pool-v3's view built a settlement's acceptance
bytes eagerly, so a decodable settlement with a zero acceptance owner (which no proof holds) threw `EncodingError`
ahead of `CONTEXT` and `PROOF`, crashing a reader where main excluded it. The settlement's messages are now built only
at recovery's settlement checks, in both views (`test/pool-v3-force.test.ts` fails without it). Its minors were taken:
a handle checks its namespace's construction at every position, and the lit cases above were added; it confirmed the
v3 equivalence line by line and found no lit soundness hole (no path for a non-owner to move, present, withdraw or
lock a note).

**Spec change:** none needed (lit-v1 §7 at `1bf5bfc` read as written).

## 2026-10-05 — Implement the lit byte layer and close four readings lit-v1 left open (slice 14 M14a)

**Status:** accepted (specification draft; reviewed). `src/lit/` implements lit-v1 §§2–5, 8–9; §6's frames, the terms
and §7 validity are M14b.

**Question:** implementing lit-v1's bytes exposed readings the draft did not fix. Which does the reference take, and is
each a change to what lit-v1 means or a statement of what it already implied?

**Decision:** four one-clause clarifications of the draft (no implementation existed before them):
1. **HKDF roots are 32 bytes, salted by the configuration hash** (§8). RFC 5869 needs an output length; pool-delivery's
   roots are 32 bytes under the same salt rule, and a different info string keeps lit's roots independent of the pool's.
2. **Two inputs with one nullifier fail the statement's own arithmetic** (§6). A nullifier is a function of the opening,
   so duplicated inputs are state-free; listing them as intrinsic gives lit the compact exclusion pool-v3 gets from its
   relation's distinct-nullifier constraint. Without it, a committed double-counting spend needed ordinary replay
   evidence to exclude.
3. **A demand's summed value must be a `u64`** (§§3, 6, 7). Every value is a `u64` (§1); a demand over two large notes
   would otherwise derive a settlement output outside it. Valid state cannot reach it (issued stays below 2^64), so it
   is a bound on hostile statements, and intrinsic.
4. **A presenter key derives from the demand's tags in its input order** (§8).

The byte layer reuses nothing of pool-v3's frames yet; it mirrors `pool/v3/records.ts` and `commitments.ts` with lit's
contexts and the evidence pair.

**Rationale:** each alternative left a reading to the implementation: an unstated HKDF length invites 64-byte roots; a
duplicate input left as non-intrinsic costs lit a compact exclusion the pool has; an unbounded demand sum yields an
opening that breaks §1. Omitting the clauses saves nothing.

**Evidence:** `test/lit-records.test.ts` against a Buffer/node:crypto oracle and `test/fixtures/lit-v1-vectors.json`
(eleven records, five publications, a key vector). One fresh adversarial review of `302e2e7` found no blocker or major:
it re-derived every length and layout, ran 60,000 mutations of the vectors (no canonicality hole, no non-`EncodingError`
throw) and raised four minors (duplicate inputs and the demand sum, above; iterator-read arrays, now `copyArray`; a
`TypeError` from `inputNullifiers`), nits (predicates answering false, the settlement's domain checked, a publication
length pre-check, the vectors' pin) and test gaps, all taken.

**Spec change:** [`fabd386`](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/commit/fabd386) (item 1) and
[`1bf5bfc`](https://github.com/mediumofexchange/money-from-first-principles/commit/1bf5bfc) (items 2–4), `lit-v1.md`.

## 2026-10-05 — Fix the lit construction's bytes: outputs derived by every reader, owner signatures over the statement, the pool's frames without a proof digest (Next 10)

**Status:** accepted (specification draft; reviewed). `lit-v1.md` is a draft until adopted; adoption needs the reference
implementation's conformance with it. No lit code yet.

**Question:** the [lit-notes design](#2026-10-05--make-the-transparent-profile-lit-notes-under-the-pools-rules-with-each-output-named-by-the-statement-that-creates-it-design-slice-next-10)
chose the transparent profile as lit notes under the pool's rules, and its item 2 lists what the layouts must carry. Which
bytes satisfy those requirements with the fewest mechanisms, and what does each pool frame become without a proof?

**Decision:** `lit-v1.md` in the specification fixes `moe/lit/v1`:
1. **Notes and derived outputs.** SHA-256 throughout, under `moe/lit/v1/` contexts. A note's commitment hashes the domain and
   its 104-byte opening (backing, value, owner key, rho). The nullifier hashes the commitment, and the tag hashes the
   nullifier. **A statement carries each output without randomness or commitment** (backing, value, owner key), and every
   reader derives rho. For a spend, burn or settlement, rho comes from the consumed nullifiers, their count and the output's
   position. For an issue, it comes from the backing, quantity, owner and a K-chosen nonce. The two derivations use
   distinct contexts. Since no statement can carry any other value, the derivation is a validity rule by construction.
   Neither derivation reads the segment. A statement signed again after a handover, return or lapse therefore keeps its
   outputs, and an issuance signed again with its nonce cannot be admitted twice.
2. **Records.** Seven kinds, numbered as the pool's. Every admitted kind binds its segment. There is no scope root, since
   the segment identity binds the scope.
   - A spend has 1–2 inputs and 1–4 outputs. A burn has 1–2 inputs and at most one change output. Every value is
     positive, and nothing is padding.
   - Derived fields are not carried: a demand's backing and quantity, and a burn's backing.
   - A record is `u32 statementLength || statement || u32 authorizationLength || authorization`, so it splits without
     decoding.
3. **Authorization.** Every statement signature is over the exact statement bytes. Their context and kind byte separate
   them from every other message a key signs.
   - K signs an issue.
   - Spend, burn, demand and request carry one owner signature per input, in input order.
   - The presenter key signs a withdrawal.
   - A settlement keeps pool-v3's 136-byte acceptance-and-release field. It repeats neither its demand's notes nor their
     owner signatures: the owners' demand names the presenter key, whose release signs the settlement (invariant 27).
     Extensions' item 2 is amended to say so.
4. **Frames.** History, evidence, snapshot, receipt, header, fault evidence, trail and package are pool-v3's under lit
   contexts. They use an evidence pair of statement and authorization digests in place of the triple, so there is no proof
   digest anywhere. The history binds the statement and the spent root, with no note root. Commitments, directories and
   record ranges are shared unchanged, and lit reads the Ergo venue profile as written. **An operator key serves one
   construction on a venue.**
5. **Compact intrinsic exclusion** (pool-v3 §9.1) covers two lit cases: a signature that fails under a key the statement
   names or under K, and a failure of the statement's own arithmetic. In both, the authorization has exactly its kind's
   length.
6. **Configuration and terms.** The configuration is `"moe/lit/v1/config" || u8(2) || u8(4)`, with hash
   `17835aa2…77c1`. Root terms keep the `MOEB` frame. The silence clause is a new tag 6 carrying the duration alone, so each
   clause tag has one payload whatever the construction. Tag 5 decides which construction decodes the terms.
7. **Wallet.** A payment request names a backing, a quantity and an owner key of its own. The payee credits each output
   to one request.
   - Owner keys derive from the seed by index (HKDF, then HMAC per index). Restoration scans with a 256-index
     look-ahead, and a wallet exposes no key beyond it. A full window, after restoration or abandoned requests, moves
     by one self-payment.
   - K's acceptance owner derives from the demand and the acceptance deadline.
   - A presenter key derives from the demand's tags, instant and deadline.
   - So restoration finds outputs, settlements to K and standing demands.
8. **Readings that differ** are named in lit §7, not branched. C3.5's `rho_out` and disclosure count retire, and so does
   C2.10.8's settlement exception. C3.8's taken release cannot occur, because equal settlement outputs need equal
   nullifiers. For the same reason, C3.4's distinct acceptance owner is not needed.

**Rationale:**
- *Carry the outputs' randomness and require the reader to check it* was the shape item 2 implied. Leaving it out gives the
  same validity rule with one consistency check fewer, and 32 bytes fewer per output.
- *Name inputs by commitment alone* would save 72 bytes an input. It would also make a statement's signature check depend
  on replayed state, which removes owner signatures from compact intrinsic exclusion and from a payee's offline check of
  a signed statement. Openings are kept.
- *Owner signatures on settlements* (the first text of Extensions' item 2) would add a second authorization of the act the
  release already authorizes. The pool's settlement proof exists to compute nullifiers and the output privately, not to
  authorize twice. In lit, both are public functions of the demand.
- *Sign the statement hash under a separate context* (pool-v3's withdrawal form) was rejected. With one signer role per
  kind, the statement's own context and kind byte already separate every role, so pool-v3's separate withdrawal message
  has no lit counterpart. A settlement's acceptance and release keep their own messages.
- *Keep the proof digest as a constant zero field* would let the v3 frames carry over verbatim. It was rejected because a
  field nothing reads is what the design refused in v3's terms (the challenge window). The receipt is 290 bytes, and the
  evidence suffix is 64 bytes per later event.
- *An output root beside the spent root* (the first draft) would have kept membership answerable without replay. No rule
  reads it: every reader of force replays (C2b.3.3), and the output set is a function of the statement order the chain
  binds. It also cost a second tree per replay and borrowed the spent tree's contexts. It was dropped, and a compact
  membership proof is a later version's.
- *Reuse the pool's silence tag 1 with a shorter payload* would make one tag mean two payloads, and a decoder could tell
  them apart only after reading tag 5.
- *Fixed input and output counts with zero padding*, as in the pool, would buy no hiding when openings are public, and
  would leave junk outputs in the set.
- *One operator key for both constructions* is cheaper by one key. It would couple four things through commitments that
  carry no construction: the sequence, the commitment in flight, receipts' `after`, and directory retention. A failure in
  one construction would then void the other's payments.
- *Reuse an owner key across requests past the look-ahead* (the first draft) was rejected. Outputs are public, so a second
  payer could present the first payer's statement as its own payment.
- *Costs:* lit's visibility, priced in Extensions. Records run 189–719 bytes, against about 15 KB for the pool. A reader
  checks one Ed25519 signature per input in place of a proof. Both are estimates, not measurements. A wallet whose
  window is full pays one self-payment fee before its next request. Limits of v1: no identified-issuance clause, no compact membership
  proof, no reliance.

**Evidence:** specification reading only; no lit code and no measurement. One fresh adversarial review of the draft
(`3fc3916`) recomputed every stated size, the configuration hash and the contexts' prefix-freeness, and found all of them
correct. It found no blocker and two majors, both resolved:
- a key shared across a pool scope and a lit scope invalidates honest receipts through `after` and couples the two
  constructions' failures (now one key per construction on a venue);
- a reused owner key lets a payer claim another payer's payment (each request now has its own key, and a payee credits an
  output once).

Its minors were also taken: the adopted block's binding exception, a release's routing beside its demand, an exact
authorization length before a compact case, venue-ergo cited for its reason, the output root dropped, the presenter key
derived for restoration, and identified issuance listed as a limit. Its nits were taken too. The review confirmed:
- holder authorization (no path lets a non-owner move, present, withdraw, lock or settle a note);
- the derivation's injectivity, and that the taken-release and distinct-owner readings hold;
- domain separation across lit, pool-v3 and the reference's contexts, and terms disjointness;
- that interleaved pool and lit checkpoints do not otherwise disturb pool classification.

Its read-back accepted every disposition and found one residual major. A restored wallet, or one whose 256 requests were
abandoned, would either reuse keys or stall. Now it treats the window as exposed and moves it by paying itself, and no
request is credited with an output of its own statement. Two smaller residuals were also taken: resumption rebuilds the
output set, and presenter keys derive from both tags, the instant and the deadline. A second read-back accepted these.
